Symmetric Lie algebras and Hermitian symmetric spaces

A symmetric space carries both a connection and an algebra of infinitesimal motions. An invariant complex structure links these two descriptions: its parallelism becomes an inner derivation, generated by a central isotropy element. We first establish this link and the decomposition needed to interpret its curvature.

All manifolds are smooth, Hausdorff and second countable. Spaces and acting groups are connected unless stated otherwise. All Lie algebras are finite dimensional and real, except where complexification is explicit. The curvature convention is \[ R(X,Y)Z=\nabla_X\nabla_YZ-\nabla_Y\nabla_XZ-\nabla_{[X,Y]}Z. \] A symmetric presentation means \[ (G^\sigma)^0\subseteq H\subseteq G^\sigma,\qquad \mathfrak g=\mathfrak h\oplus\mathfrak m, \] where \(\sigma\) is an involution and the summands are the positive and negative eigenspaces of its differential \(\theta\). The canonical connection and all quotient conventions are those proved in Symmetric spaces B.1–B.2. In particular, \[ R(X,Y)Z=-[[X,Y],Z]\quad(X,Y,Z\in\mathfrak m). \tag{0.1} \]

A. Complex structures preserved by reflections

A complex structure is a smooth endomorphism \(J\) with \(J^2=-I\) that is induced by a complex coordinate atlas. A smooth map between complex manifolds is holomorphic when its differential is complex linear in these charts: real differentiability with complex-linear derivative is precisely complex differentiability. A Hermitian metric is a Riemannian metric with \(g(JX,JY)=g(X,Y)\). Put \(\omega(X,Y)=g(JX,Y)\).

Lemma A.1 (The two Kähler conditions). For a Hermitian metric on a complex manifold, \[ d\omega=0\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\nabla J=0, \tag{A.1} \] where \(\nabla\) is the Levi-Civita connection. Either condition defines a Kähler metric.

Proof. If \(J\) is parallel, metric compatibility makes \(\omega\) parallel. For a two-form and a torsion-free connection, expansion of the definition of the exterior derivative, replacing brackets by differences of covariant derivatives, gives \[ d\omega(X,Y,Z)= (\nabla_X\omega)(Y,Z)+(\nabla_Y\omega)(Z,X)+(\nabla_Z\omega)(X,Y). \] Thus \(d\omega=0\).

Conversely work in complex coordinates \(z^1,\ldots,z^k\), and extend the metric and connection complex bilinearly. Write \[ \partial_a=\tfrac12(\partial_{x^a}-i\partial_{y^a}),\qquad \partial_{\bar a}=\tfrac12(\partial_{x^a}+i\partial_{y^a}). \] These fields commute. Hermitian compatibility gives \(g_{ab}=g_{\bar a\bar b}=0\), while the matrix \(g_{a\bar b}\) is nondegenerate. Since \(J\partial_a=i\partial_a\) and \(J\partial_{\bar a}=-i\partial_{\bar a}\), closure of \(\omega\), evaluated on \(\partial_a,\partial_b,\partial_{\bar c}\) and their conjugates, gives \[ \partial_a g_{b\bar c}=\partial_b g_{a\bar c},\qquad \partial_{\bar a}g_{c\bar b}=\partial_{\bar b}g_{c\bar a}. \tag{A.2} \] The complex-bilinear extension of the Koszul formula proved in Symmetric spaces A.1 now yields \[ \begin{aligned} 2g(\nabla_{\partial_{\bar c}}\partial_a,\partial_b) &=\partial_a g_{b\bar c}-\partial_b g_{a\bar c}=0,\\ 2g(\nabla_{\partial_{\bar c}}\partial_a,\partial_{\bar b}) &=\partial_{\bar c}g_{a\bar b}-\partial_{\bar b}g_{a\bar c}=0. \end{aligned} \] Hence the mixed derivative is zero. Also \(g(\nabla_{\partial_c}\partial_a,\partial_b)=0\), because every term in its Koszul formula involves a zero pure-type metric coefficient. Nondegeneracy of the mixed matrix says that \(\nabla_{\partial_c}\partial_a\) has type \((1,0)\). Conjugation gives the corresponding assertions for type \((0,1)\). The connection preserves both eigensubbundles of \(J\), which is exactly \(\nabla J=0\). These are coordinate identities and therefore hold on the manifold. □

Theorem A.2 (Hermitian symmetry and parallelism). A Hermitian symmetric space is Kähler. Conversely, a Kähler structure on a connected Riemannian symmetric space makes every point symmetry holomorphic.

Proof. A Hermitian symmetric space has, at every point \(p\), a holomorphic isometry \(s_p\) fixing \(p\) with derivative \(-I\). An isometry preserves the Levi-Civita connection by Riemannian connections A.3, and holomorphicity preserves \(J\). It therefore preserves the tensor \((\nabla_XJ)Y\), by the tensor-derivative rule in Linear connections C.1. At \(p\), its two inputs acquire two minus signs and its output one. Thus \[ -(\nabla_XJ)Y=(\nabla_{-X}J)(-Y)=(\nabla_XJ)Y, \] so \(\nabla J=0\) everywhere. Lemma A.1 gives the first conclusion.

Conversely, a point symmetry is an isometry and hence affine. Its pullback of the parallel tensor \(J\) is parallel. At its centre this pullback equals \(J\), since \(-I\) commutes with \(J\). Two parallel tensors agreeing at one point agree along every piecewise smooth path: their components in parallel frames satisfy the same linear ODE, as in Linear connections C.1 and Connections D.2. The set reachable from a point by piecewise smooth paths is open, and its complement is open, since coordinate balls join their points by straight coordinate segments. Connectedness makes every point reachable. The pullback is therefore \(J\) everywhere. Its differential is complex linear, which proves holomorphicity. □

Theorem A.3 (The full stabilizer condition). On a symmetric presentation, an endomorphism \(I\) of \(\mathfrak m\) determines a \(G\)-invariant complex structure exactly when \[ I^2=-I_{\mathfrak m},\qquad I\operatorname{Ad}(h)|_{\mathfrak m} =\operatorname{Ad}(h)|_{\mathfrak m}I\quad(h\in H). \tag{A.3} \] Here \(I_{\mathfrak m}\) denotes the identity operator. Every positive \(H\)-invariant inner product compatible with this complex structure gives a complete Hermitian symmetric Kähler metric. Given a positive invariant inner product \(b\), a compatible one is \[ b_I(X,Y)=\tfrac12\bigl(b(X,Y)+b(IX,IY)\bigr). \tag{A.4} \]

Proof. Translating \(I\) from the origin defines a tensor precisely when changing a coset representative by \(h\in H\) has no effect. This is the second condition in (A.3), by the associated-tensor construction in Homogeneous spaces C.1. The first condition is the almost complex condition.

Every bracket of two vectors in \(\mathfrak m\) lies in \(\mathfrak h\). Consequently all four \(\mathfrak m\)-bracket terms in Homogeneous spaces G.4 vanish. That theorem constructs complex coordinates for the invariant almost complex structure, with a complete analytic Frobenius proof. It also proves that the \(G\)-action is holomorphic. This establishes sufficiency for integrability without requiring a separate unproved integrability theorem. Necessity follows by evaluating an invariant complex structure at the origin.

The form (A.4) is positive, symmetric and invariant, and replacing both arguments by their \(I\)-images leaves it unchanged. A compatible invariant metric has the canonical Levi-Civita connection by Symmetric spaces B.2. The same theorem makes every invariant tensor, in particular \(J\), parallel and makes the connection complete. Its global point symmetries are isometries; Theorem A.2 makes them holomorphic, and Lemma A.1 makes the metric Kähler. Every condition has been imposed on the full \(H\), including any disconnected components. □

B. Solvable ideals and traces

Write \(B(X,Y)=\operatorname{tr}_{\mathfrak g}(\operatorname{ad}X\,\operatorname{ad}Y)\) for the Killing form and \(\mathfrak r\) for the solvable radical. Solvability, radicals and finite-dimensional representations were developed with proofs in Symmetric spaces H.2–H.6.

Lemma B.1 (A solvable ideal on an irreducible module). If \(\mathfrak a\) is a solvable ideal in a complex Lie algebra \(\mathfrak g\), then on any nonzero finite-dimensional irreducible complex \(\mathfrak g\)-module \(V\), every element of \(\mathfrak a\) acts by a scalar and \([\mathfrak g,\mathfrak a]\) acts by zero.

Proof. Lie's theorem, Symmetric spaces H.3, supplies a nonzero vector \(v\) and a linear functional \(\lambda\) on \(\mathfrak a\) with \(yv=\lambda(y)v\). There are only finitely many possible functionals of common eigenvectors: triangularize the action of \(\mathfrak a\); the last nonzero coordinate of any common eigenvector identifies its functional with one of the finitely many diagonal functionals.

Fix \(x\in\mathfrak g\). The ideal \(\mathfrak a\) is stable under \(\operatorname{ad}x\) and its exponential. The finite-dimensional matrix exponential identity \[ e^{t\rho(x)}\rho(y)e^{-t\rho(x)} =\rho(e^{t\operatorname{ad}x}y) \tag{B.1} \] follows by differentiating both sides; they solve the same linear commutator ODE with the same initial value, using Local tools 2.3. Thus \(e^{t\rho(x)}v\) is a common eigenvector for \(\mathfrak a\), with functional \(\lambda\circ e^{-t\operatorname{ad}x}\). For real \(t\) this is a continuous map into the finite set of diagonal functionals. It is constant, since an interval is connected. Differentiating at zero gives \(\lambda([x,y])=0\) for every \(y\in\mathfrak a\).

Let \(W\) be the common eigenspace for \(\lambda\), containing \(v\). For \(w\in W\), \[ \rho(y)\rho(x)w =\rho(x)\rho(y)w+\rho([y,x])w =\lambda(y)\rho(x)w. \] The last equality uses \([y,x]\in\mathfrak a\) and its zero \(\lambda\)-value. Thus \(W\) is \(\mathfrak g\)-invariant. Irreducibility gives \(W=V\), proving the scalar assertion and the vanishing of the commutators. □

Theorem B.2 (The radical from the Killing form). For every real or complex Lie algebra, \[ \mathfrak r =\{X\in\mathfrak g:B(X,[\mathfrak g,\mathfrak g])=0\}. \tag{B.2} \] More generally, in any finite-dimensional representation \(\rho\), a solvable ideal \(\mathfrak a\) satisfies \[ \operatorname{tr}\bigl(\rho(X)\rho(Y)\bigr)=0 \quad(X\in\mathfrak a,\ Y\in[\mathfrak g,\mathfrak g]). \tag{B.3} \]

Proof. Over \(\mathbb C\), choose a finite invariant filtration of the module with irreducible quotients. It exists by choosing a nonzero invariant subspace of least dimension, then proceeding in the quotient; dimension decreases at every step. On each irreducible quotient, Lemma B.1 makes \(\rho(X)\) a scalar. The trace of \(\rho(Y)\) there is zero because \(Y\) is a sum of brackets, each represented by a matrix commutator. Hence the trace of their product is zero. A basis adapted to the filtration makes both operators block upper triangular, so the trace of their product is the sum of these quotient traces. This proves (B.3). For real algebras and modules, complexify the matrices and the solvable ideal. Solvability is preserved by complexification by Symmetric spaces H.2, and matrix traces are unchanged; the same conclusion follows.

Apply (B.3) to the adjoint representation and \(\mathfrak a=\mathfrak r\). This proves one inclusion in (B.2). Denote the space on its right by \(\mathfrak b\). Killing-form invariance and the fact that the derived algebra is an ideal give \[ B([Z,X],Y)=-B(X,[Z,Y])=0 \quad(X\in\mathfrak b,\ Y\in[\mathfrak g,\mathfrak g]). \] Thus \(\mathfrak b\) is an ideal. For any ideal, restriction of the ambient Killing form to it equals its own Killing form, as proved in Symmetric spaces H.5. Since \([\mathfrak b,\mathfrak b]\subseteq[\mathfrak g,\mathfrak g]\), we have \[ B_{\mathfrak b}(\mathfrak b,[\mathfrak b,\mathfrak b])=0. \] The solvability criterion in that same earlier theorem makes \(\mathfrak b\) solvable. Maximality of the radical gives \(\mathfrak b\subseteq\mathfrak r\), proving equality. □

C. Orthogonal symmetric algebras

A symmetric Lie algebra is a real Lie algebra with an involution \(\theta\), whose eigenspaces we denote by \(\mathfrak h,\mathfrak m\). Thus \[ [\mathfrak h,\mathfrak h]\subseteq\mathfrak h,\qquad [\mathfrak h,\mathfrak m]\subseteq\mathfrak m,\qquad [\mathfrak m,\mathfrak m]\subseteq\mathfrak h. \] It is orthogonal here if some positive inner product on \(\mathfrak g\) makes every \(\operatorname{ad}A\), \(A\in\mathfrak h\), skew-adjoint. In particular this condition holds when the connected linear group generated by these adjoint operators is compact: average a positive form using Symmetric spaces D.1. Averaging the form with its transform under \(\theta\) also makes the involution orthogonal. The arguments below require only this invariant-form condition, so they also apply when that generated group merely has compact closure.

The pair is effective if no nonzero ideal lies in \(\mathfrak h\). It has Euclidean type if it is effective and \([\mathfrak m,\mathfrak m]=0\). A semisimple pair has compact type when \(B\) is negative definite on \(\mathfrak g\). An effective semisimple pair has noncompact type when \[ B_\theta(X,Y)=-B(X,\theta Y) \tag{C.1} \] is positive definite. A pair with zero algebra is allowed as an empty summand.

Lemma C.1 (Faithfulness and the even Killing form). The kernel of the action of \(\mathfrak h\) on \(\mathfrak m\) is exactly the largest ideal contained in \(\mathfrak h\). Thus effectiveness is equivalent to faithfulness of that action. On an orthogonal pair, \[ B(\mathfrak h,\mathfrak m)=0,\qquad B(A,A)=-\|\operatorname{ad}A\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2 \quad(A\in\mathfrak h), \tag{C.2} \] where the norm is taken for an invariant positive form on \(\mathfrak g\). In particular \(B|_{\mathfrak h}\) is negative definite if \(\mathfrak h\cap Z(\mathfrak g)=0\), and hence if the pair is effective.

Proof. Let \(\mathfrak k\) be the kernel in question. For \(A\in\mathfrak k\), \(D\in\mathfrak h\), \(X\in\mathfrak m\), Jacobi gives \([[D,A],X]=[D,[A,X]]-[A,[D,X]]=0\). Thus \([\mathfrak h,\mathfrak k]\subseteq\mathfrak k\), and by definition \([\mathfrak m,\mathfrak k]=0\). This makes \(\mathfrak k\) an ideal. Conversely an ideal contained in \(\mathfrak h\) brackets with \(\mathfrak m\) into both eigenspaces, hence into zero, so is contained in \(\mathfrak k\).

Every automorphism preserves the Killing form, since it conjugates adjoint operators. Applying \(\theta\) to one even and one odd argument reverses their pairing, proving the first equality of (C.2). In an orthonormal basis a skew matrix \(T\) satisfies \(\operatorname{tr}T^2=-\operatorname{tr}(T^TT)=-\sum_{i,j}T_{ij}^2\). Take \(T=\operatorname{ad}A\). Equality to zero means that \(A\) is central. The stated intersection condition therefore gives negative definiteness. In an effective pair any central even line is an ideal in \(\mathfrak h\), so that intersection is zero. □

Theorem C.2 (Decomposition by the Killing operator). An effective orthogonal symmetric Lie algebra is a direct sum of symmetric ideals \[ \mathfrak g=\mathfrak g_0\oplus\mathfrak g_c\oplus\mathfrak g_n. \tag{C.3} \] The first is a Euclidean pair \(\mathfrak g_0=\mathfrak h_0\ltimes\mathfrak m_0\) with faithful skew-adjoint isotropy. The second and third are semisimple of compact and noncompact type. Each semisimple part splits further into effective symmetric ideals with irreducible nonzero odd representation. If \(\mathfrak m\) generates \(\mathfrak g\), then \(\mathfrak h_0=0\) and the Euclidean ideal consists only of translations.

Proof. Restrict an invariant positive form to \(\mathfrak m\), writing it as \(\langle\, ,\,\rangle\). Define its self-adjoint Killing operator \(S\) by \[ B(X,Y)=\langle SX,Y\rangle\quad(X,Y\in\mathfrak m). \] Invariance of both forms makes \(S\) commute with every \(\operatorname{ad}A|_{\mathfrak m}\). The real spectral theorem proved in De Rham decomposition H.2 gives an orthogonal splitting into invariant eigenspaces \(\mathfrak m=\bigoplus_\lambda V_\lambda\). The corresponding Killing forms are \(\lambda\langle\, ,\,\rangle\).

For distinct eigenvalues, \(X\in V_\lambda,Y\in V_\mu\) and \(A\in\mathfrak h\), Killing invariance gives \[ B([X,Y],A)=B(X,[Y,A])=0, \] since \([Y,A]\in V_\mu\). Nondegeneracy of \(B|_{\mathfrak h}\), from C.1, proves \([V_\lambda,V_\mu]=0\). The same calculation, now using \(B(V_0,\mathfrak m)=0\), proves \[ [V_0,\mathfrak m]=0. \tag{C.4} \]

For \(\lambda\ne0\), set \(\mathfrak h_\lambda=[V_\lambda,V_\lambda]\). Jacobi shows that it is an ideal in \(\mathfrak h\), preserves \(V_\lambda\), and acts trivially on every other \(V_\mu\). Distinct \(\mathfrak h_\lambda\)'s commute: expand the bracket of an element of \([V_\lambda,V_\lambda]\) with one of \([V_\mu,V_\mu]\), and use the vanishing cross brackets. They have zero intersections and independent sum, since an element in the intersection of one with the sum of the others acts trivially on all of \(\mathfrak m\), so is zero by faithfulness.

They are also pairwise \(B\)-orthogonal: for \(A\in\mathfrak h_\mu\), \(B([X,Y],A)=B(X,[Y,A])=0\) when \(X,Y\in V_\lambda\), \(\lambda\ne\mu\). Let \(\mathfrak h_0\) be their orthogonal complement inside the negative-definite space \((\mathfrak h,B)\). Invariance makes it an ideal of \(\mathfrak h\). Orthogonal complementary ideals commute, because their bracket belongs to both. For \(A\in\mathfrak h_0\), \(X,Y\in V_\lambda\), \(\lambda\ne0\), \[ B([A,X],Y)=B(A,[X,Y])=0. \] The action preserves \(V_\lambda\), where \(B\) is nondegenerate, so \([\mathfrak h_0,V_\lambda]=0\).

Consequently \[ \mathfrak g_\lambda=\mathfrak h_\lambda\oplus V_\lambda \quad(\lambda\ne0),\qquad \mathfrak g_0=\mathfrak h_0\oplus V_0 \] are mutually commuting, involution-stable ideals whose sum is all of \(\mathfrak g\). Equation (C.4) makes \(\mathfrak g_0\) the asserted semidirect product; its isotropy is faithful since an element acting trivially on \(V_0\) acts trivially on every summand.

On \(\mathfrak g_\lambda\), the Killing form is the restriction of \(B\), either by the ideal formula of Symmetric spaces H.5 or by the commuting direct-sum matrices. It is nondegenerate: its even part is negative definite, its odd part is the nonzero multiple \(\lambda\langle\, ,\,\rangle\), and these parts are orthogonal. That earlier Killing criterion makes \(\mathfrak g_\lambda\) semisimple. It has compact type if \(\lambda<0\), and noncompact type if \(\lambda>0\), by (C.1).

For the final irreducibility statement, decompose any \(V_\lambda\), \(\lambda\ne0\), into irreducible \(\mathfrak h\)-invariant real subspaces. This needs no general representation theorem: an invariant subspace has invariant orthogonal complement because the action is skew, so induction on dimension supplies the decomposition. For two orthogonal invariant pieces \(V,W\) of the same eigenspace, the calculation \(B([V,W],A)=B(V,[W,A])=0\) again gives \([V,W]=0\). Repeating the preceding bracket argument splits \(\mathfrak g_\lambda\) into the ideals \([V,V]\oplus V\). There is no residual even part because \(\mathfrak h_\lambda=[V_\lambda,V_\lambda]\). Each such piece is effective, and its odd representation is irreducible by construction. It is nontrivial: if \([\,\mathfrak h,V\,]=0\), the same Killing pairing would give \([V,\mathfrak m]=0\), so \(V\) would be central, contradicting the nondegeneracy on that piece.

Finally the subalgebra generated by \(\mathfrak m\) is \([\mathfrak m,\mathfrak m]\oplus\mathfrak m\): Jacobi and the symmetric bracket rules make this a subalgebra. Its even part is the sum of the \(\mathfrak h_\lambda\), \(\lambda\ne0\). If it is all of \(\mathfrak g\), its complementary \(\mathfrak h_0\) must vanish. □

Corollary C.3 (The central-kernel hypothesis). The decomposition into Euclidean, compact and noncompact symmetric ideals also holds for an orthogonal pair satisfying \[ \mathfrak h\cap Z(\mathfrak g)=0. \tag{C.5} \] The compact ideal may additionally contain semisimple ideals entirely in \(\mathfrak h\), contributing no tangent directions. Removing those ideals leaves the effective decomposition of C.2.

Proof. Let \(\mathfrak k\) be the even ideal from C.1. Restrict the invariant positive form on \(\mathfrak g\) to \(\mathfrak h\). The orthogonal complement of \(\mathfrak k\) in \(\mathfrak h\) is an ideal, by invariance, and the two ideals commute. If \(Z\) lies in the centre of \(\mathfrak k\), it therefore commutes with all of \(\mathfrak h\); it also commutes with \(\mathfrak m\) by the definition of \(\mathfrak k\). Condition (C.5) gives \(Z=0\).

The adjoint operators of \(\mathfrak k\) on itself are skew and its centre is zero. The matrix calculation in C.1 makes its own Killing form negative definite. By Symmetric spaces H.5 it is semisimple. Its Killing form equals \(B|_{\mathfrak k}\), because it is an ideal of \(\mathfrak g\). Thus its \(B\)-orthogonal complement in \(\mathfrak g\) is an ideal complementary to it: solving the nondegenerate pairing on \(\mathfrak k\) gives the direct sum, even if \(B\) is degenerate elsewhere. The ideals commute and are involution stable. Every odd vector belongs to this complement by (C.2).

The complementary pair is effective. An even element in its isotropy kernel would belong to \(\mathfrak k\) by C.1 and also to its complementary ideal, hence be zero. Apply C.2 to it, and add the compact, purely even ideal \(\mathfrak k\) to the compact summand. □

Proposition C.4 (Realizing a semisimple pair). Every semisimple orthogonal symmetric Lie algebra has a connected Lie-group presentation with closed connected isotropy and the prescribed Lie algebra and involution.

Proof. Inside \(GL(\mathfrak g)\), let \[ \operatorname{Aut}(\mathfrak g) =\{F:F[X,Y]=[FX,FY]\text{ for all }X,Y\}. \] Checking on a basis expresses this set by finitely many polynomial equations, so it is closed relative to \(GL(\mathfrak g)\). Invariant connections A.1 makes it an embedded Lie group. Differentiation of its equations shows that its Lie algebra lies in \(\operatorname{Der}(\mathfrak g)\). Conversely a derivation \(D\) satisfies \[ \frac{d}{dt}\left(e^{-tD}[e^{tD}X,e^{tD}Y]\right)=0 \] by the derivation identity. Hence \(e^{tD}\) is an automorphism, proving equality of these Lie algebras. Symmetric spaces H.6 identifies \(\operatorname{Der}(\mathfrak g)\) with \(\operatorname{ad}\mathfrak g\), faithfully since the centre is zero.

Take \(G=\operatorname{Aut}(\mathfrak g)^0\) and let its involution be \(F\mapsto\theta F\theta^{-1}\). Under the adjoint identification its differential is \(\theta\). The identity component \(H\) of its fixed subgroup is closed and has Lie algebra \(\mathfrak h\), by Symmetric spaces B.1 and the closedness of components. Its action preserves the given positive form: each \(\exp(\operatorname{ad}A)\), \(A\in\mathfrak h\), preserves it, and these exponentials generate connected \(H\), by Flat connections A.1. Thus this is a symmetric presentation with a positive invariant tangent metric. □

D. Metrics, curvature and the dual pair

Theorem D.1 (Every invariant metric and the Ricci trace). For an effective semisimple orthogonal pair, let \(\mathfrak g=\bigoplus_a\mathfrak g_a\) be its decomposition from C.2 into irreducible symmetric ideals, with odd spaces \(V_a\). Every positive infinitesimally invariant inner product on \(\mathfrak m\) is an orthogonal sum \[ b|_{V_a}=c_a\epsilon_a B|_{V_a},\qquad c_a>0, \tag{D.1} \] where \(\epsilon_a=-1\) on compact factors and \(+1\) on noncompact factors. For an actual \(G/H\), full \(H\)-invariance must also hold; in particular, components permuted by \(H\) must have the corresponding equal scales.

For its canonical metric connection, \[ \operatorname{Ric}(X,Y)=-\tfrac12 B(X,Y). \tag{D.2} \] On one irreducible summand write \(B=\lambda b\), with \(\lambda\ne0\). Then \[ b(R(X,Y)Y,X)=\lambda^{-1}B([X,Y],[X,Y]) \quad(X,Y\in V_a). \tag{D.3} \] Compact factors have nonnegative sectional and positive Ricci curvature; noncompact factors have nonpositive sectional and negative Ricci curvature.

Proof. Fix one reference invariant positive form and its irreducible splitting from C.2. The Lie algebra \(\mathfrak h_a=[V_a,V_a]\) acts trivially on \(V_b\) for \(b\ne a\), and nontrivially on \(V_a\). The span \([\mathfrak h_a,V_a]\) is invariant and nonzero, so irreducibility makes it all of \(V_a\). For any other invariant bilinear form \(b\), \[ b([A,X],Y)=-b(X,[A,Y])=0 \quad(A\in\mathfrak h_a,\ X\in V_a,\ Y\in V_b). \] Thus the distinct summands are orthogonal for \(b\) too.

On \(V_a\), the self-adjoint operator comparing two positive invariant forms commutes with the isotropy action. Every eigenspace is invariant; irreducibility and De Rham decomposition H.2 make this operator scalar. Applying this to the positive definite form \(\epsilon_a B\) proves (D.1). An automorphism in the full stabilizer preserves \(B\) and sends each irreducible ideal to an ideal of the same type, so its invariance imposes the stated equality when it permutes factors.

For the trace computation, \(\operatorname{ad}X\) exchanges \(\mathfrak h\) and \(\mathfrak m\) when \(X\in\mathfrak m\). Put \(a_X=\operatorname{ad}X|_{\mathfrak h}:\mathfrak h\to\mathfrak m\) and \(b_X=\operatorname{ad}X|_{\mathfrak m}:\mathfrak m\to\mathfrak h\). The entry-sum identity for rectangular matrices gives \(\operatorname{tr}_{\mathfrak h}(b_Xa_Y)=\operatorname{tr}_{\mathfrak m}(a_Yb_X)\). Also \[ \operatorname{tr}_{\mathfrak m}(a_Xb_Y-a_Yb_X) =\operatorname{tr}_{\mathfrak m}\operatorname{ad}[X,Y]=0, \] because the even action is skew-adjoint. Hence \[ B(X,Y)=2\operatorname{tr}_{\mathfrak m}(a_Yb_X). \] Formula (0.1) gives \(R(Z,X)Y=-a_Yb_XZ\). Its trace over \(Z\) is the definition of Ricci, as in Sectional curvature A.1. This proves (D.2).

Finally Killing invariance yields \[ \begin{aligned} \lambda b(R(X,Y)Y,X) &=B(-[[X,Y],Y],X)\\ &=B([X,Y],[X,Y]), \end{aligned} \] proving (D.3). The bracket is even, where \(B\) is negative definite by C.1. Thus its sign is nonnegative when \(\lambda<0\) and nonpositive when \(\lambda>0\). On a sum of ideals, cross brackets and cross curvature vanish, so the numerator is the sum of those on the individual factors. The Ricci signs follow directly from (D.2) and definiteness of \(B\) on the appropriate odd summands. □

Theorem D.2 (Global geometry of the two types). A connected Riemannian symmetric space with effective orthogonal algebra of compact type is compact and has finite fundamental group. One of noncompact type is simply connected, and every exponential map is a diffeomorphism from its tangent space onto the space. These assertions allow reducible spaces and every invariant positive metric.

Proof. Symmetric spaces A.2 proves completeness and homogeneity, and D.1 gives the curvature signs and definite Ricci tensors. In compact type, the finite positive scales in (D.1) give a uniform constant \(c>0\) with \(\operatorname{Ric}\geq c\,b\): on factor \(a\), this constant is \(1/(2c_a)\), so take the least of these finitely many numbers. For positive dimension \(n\), necessarily \(n\geq2\), since the curvature and Ricci of a one-dimensional manifold vanish by skewness of the curvature tensor, contradicting positive Ricci. Comparison D.1 applies with \(k=c/(n-1)\), proving compactness and finite fundamental group.

In noncompact type, D.1 gives nonpositive sectional curvature and negative-definite Ricci. Comparison H.2 proves that every connected homogeneous manifold with these properties is simply connected and has global exponential diffeomorphisms. Its proof includes the universal-cover and constant-displacement arguments, so no simple-connectivity assumption is added here. If the odd space is zero, the connected homogeneous space is a point, and the conclusions have their evident zero-dimensional meaning. □

Proposition D.3 (Duality with the metric fixed). Inside the complexification of a symmetric Lie algebra, set \[ \mathfrak g^*=\mathfrak h\oplus i\mathfrak m. \tag{D.4} \] This is a real symmetric Lie algebra. Duality preserves orthogonality and effectiveness, is involutive up to its displayed identification, and exchanges compact and noncompact type for semisimple effective pairs. It preserves the set of infinitesimally invariant complex structures on the odd space. Under \(X\leftrightarrow iX\), with the same positive tangent metric, the dual canonical curvature is the negative of the original.

Proof. Complex-bilinear extension of the bracket gives \[ [A,B]^*=[A,B],\qquad [A,iX]^*=i[A,X],\qquad [iX,iY]^*=-[X,Y]. \] These formulas close on (D.4). Jacobi holds because it holds in the complexification. The extension of \(\theta\) preserves this subspace with the indicated positive and negative eigenspaces. Applying the construction again gives \(\mathfrak h\oplus(-\mathfrak m)=\mathfrak g\), with its original bracket.

The even representation on the whole vector space, identified by \(X\leftrightarrow iX\), is unchanged. Thus its invariant positive form transfers, and so does faithfulness on the odd space. Lemma C.1 proves preservation of effectiveness.

Killing forms are traces of the same complexified adjoint matrices, so their restrictions satisfy \[ B^*(A,B)=B(A,B),\quad B^*(iX,iY)=-B(X,Y),\quad B^*(\mathfrak h,i\mathfrak m)=0. \tag{D.5} \] These identities preserve nondegeneracy and exchange the two definiteness conditions. By the Killing criterion in Symmetric spaces H.5 the dual remains semisimple.

The operator \(I^*(iX)=iIX\) has square minus the identity and commutes with the even action exactly when \(I\) does. Compatibility with the transferred positive metric is also identical. This is an infinitesimal statement; an actual global presentation must satisfy the full stabilizer condition (A.3).

Substitute the brackets into (0.1): \[ R^*(iX,iY)iZ=-[\, -[X,Y],iZ\,] =i[[X,Y],Z]=-iR(X,Y)Z. \tag{D.6} \] The tangent identification is isometric by the choice of metric, so this reverses every curvature numerator and the Ricci tensor. Semisimple realizations with connected isotropy are supplied by C.4 for either pair. □

E. The central isotropy element

Theorem E.1 (A complex structure is an inner derivation). Let \(G/H\) be a connected symmetric isometry presentation of a Hermitian symmetric space whose Lie-algebra pair is effective and semisimple. Its complex structure is \(G\)-invariant, and its value at the origin is generated by a unique \(Z\in\mathfrak z(\mathfrak h)\): \[ \operatorname{ad}Z|_{\mathfrak m}=I,\qquad C_{\mathfrak g}(Z)=\mathfrak h. \tag{E.1} \] Every member of the full stabilizer fixes this element: \(\operatorname{Ad}(h)Z=Z\) for \(h\in H\).

Proof. The even action on the metric odd space is skew and faithful, by C.1. Pulling back the positive trace form on skew operators gives a positive inner product on the even space: \[ q(A,B)=-\operatorname{tr}_{\mathfrak m} \bigl(\operatorname{ad}A|_{\mathfrak m}\operatorname{ad}B|_{\mathfrak m}\bigr). \] Cyclicity of trace makes this form invariant under the even adjoint action. Its orthogonal sum with the given odd metric therefore makes the pair orthogonal in the sense of Part C.

By A.2 the complex structure is parallel and every point symmetry is holomorphic. In C.2 the Euclidean ideal of a semisimple algebra must vanish: its abelian odd ideal is zero by semisimplicity, and faithfulness then kills its even part. Thus \(\mathfrak m\) generates \(\mathfrak g\). For \(X\in\mathfrak m\), the action of \(\exp(tX)\) is the transvection along \(\gamma(t)=\exp(tX)H\): Symmetric spaces A.2 and B.2 give the same value and parallel-transport differential, and Affine transformations A.1 gives equality of the global affine maps. That transvection is a product of two point symmetries, hence preserves \(J\). The infinitesimal action of \(\mathfrak m\) therefore preserves \(J\); so do its brackets, by expanding commutators of Lie derivatives. Since these generate \(\mathfrak g\), all its infinitesimal actions preserve \(J\). Their flows, and hence connected \(G\), preserve \(J\), by exponential generation in Flat connections A.1. This proves the asserted invariance.

The commutator defining curvature, applied to the parallel tensor \(J\), gives \([R(X,Y),I]=0\) at the origin. The Riemannian curvature symmetries, proved in Sectional curvature A.1, and orthogonality of \(I\) imply \[ \begin{aligned} b(R(IX,IY)U,V) &=b(R(U,V)IX,IY)\\ &=b(IR(U,V)X,IY)\\ &=b(R(U,V)X,Y) =b(R(X,Y)U,V). \end{aligned} \] Nondegeneracy gives \(R(IX,IY)=R(X,Y)\). The curvature formula (0.1) and faithfulness of the even action from C.1 yield \[ [IX,IY]=[X,Y]. \] Replacing \(Y\) by \(IY\) gives \[ [IX,Y]+[X,IY]=0. \tag{E.2} \]

Define \(D|_{\mathfrak h}=0\) and \(D|_{\mathfrak m}=I\). It is a derivation. For two even arguments this is immediate; for one even and one odd it is the infinitesimal invariance of \(I\); for two odd arguments it is (E.2). Symmetric spaces H.6, whose proof does not assume this geometric assertion, supplies a unique \(Z\in\mathfrak g\) with \(D=\operatorname{ad}Z\).

Since \(D\) commutes with \(\theta\), \(\operatorname{ad}(\theta Z)=\theta D\theta^{-1}=D\). The zero centre of the semisimple algebra gives \(\theta Z=Z\), hence \(Z\in\mathfrak h\). Its zero action on \(\mathfrak h\) makes it central there. An element \(A+X\), \(A\in\mathfrak h,X\in\mathfrak m\), commutes with \(Z\) exactly when \(IX=0\), hence exactly when \(X=0\). This proves the centralizer equality.

For \(h\in H\), the adjoint action preserves both eigenspaces and commutes with \(I\) on the odd one, by (A.3). It therefore commutes with \(D\) on all of \(\mathfrak g\). Conjugating the inner derivation gives \(\operatorname{ad}(\operatorname{Ad}(h)Z)=D\). Uniqueness of the inner generator proves the last assertion, including disconnected stabilizers. □

Theorem E.2 (The irreducible central-circle criterion). Let an effective semisimple orthogonal pair have a nonzero irreducible odd representation. Its connected-isotropy presentation is Hermitian symmetric exactly when \(\mathfrak z(\mathfrak h)\ne0\). In this case that centre is one dimensional, and there are exactly two infinitesimally invariant complex structures, \(I\) and \(-I\). They are compatible with every invariant positive tangent metric and transfer to the dual pair. Their adjoint rotations form a circle. Descent through any further disconnected stabilizer is still governed by (A.3).

Proof. A Hermitian structure gives the nonzero central element of E.1, so proves necessity. Conversely take \(0\ne Z\in\mathfrak z(\mathfrak h)\), and let \(A=\operatorname{ad}Z|_{\mathfrak m}\). Faithfulness makes \(A\ne0\). It is skew-adjoint for an invariant positive metric and commutes with the isotropy. Its square is self-adjoint and commutes with the isotropy, so its eigenspaces are invariant. Irreducibility and De Rham decomposition H.2 give \[ A^2=-c^2I_{\mathfrak m},\qquad c>0. \] Indeed the scalar is nonpositive since \(\langle A^2X,X\rangle=-|AX|^2\), and scalar zero would force \(A=0\). Thus \(I=A/c\) is an orthogonal complex structure commuting with the even action. For connected isotropy this is full invariance, since exponentials generate that group. Theorem A.3 supplies the Hermitian symmetric metric. Existence of an actual such presentation, rather than only an abstract algebra, is C.4.

If \(W\) is another central even element, its odd action \(A_W\) commutes with \(I\). Both are skew-adjoint, so \(IA_W\) is self-adjoint. It commutes with the isotropy and is therefore a scalar. Multiplication by \(-I\) says \(A_W\) is a scalar multiple of \(I\). Faithfulness then says that \(W\) is a scalar multiple of \(Z\). The centre is one dimensional.

Any infinitesimally invariant complex structure \(J\) can first be made orthogonal using (A.4); connected-isotropy invariance and A.3 then give a Hermitian symmetric structure. Theorem E.1 supplies a central element generating it. The one-dimensional centre makes \(J=tI\), and \(J^2=-I_{\mathfrak m}\) gives \(t=\pm1\). Finally D.1 makes every invariant positive metric on this irreducible odd space a positive scalar multiple of any one such metric, so these two structures are orthogonal for all of them.

Normalize the central generator so that its odd action is \(I\). Its adjoint exponential is the identity on \(\mathfrak h\) and \[ e^{tI}=(\cos t)I_{\mathfrak m}+(\sin t)I \] on \(\mathfrak m\), by separating the even and odd terms of the exponential series. The circle parameter proved in Connections E.1 shows that its least positive period is \(2\pi\), since \(\mathfrak m\ne0\) and \(I\) is not a real scalar. Its image is a compact circle of automorphisms. Proposition D.3 transfers the two complex structures to the dual. A further global quotient preserves one of them precisely when its full stabilizer commutes with it, by A.3. □

F. Two compact-group foundations

Lemma F.1 (The universal covering group). The connected universal cover of a connected Lie group has a unique Lie-group structure for which its based covering map is a homomorphism. The map identifies the Lie algebras and has discrete central kernel.

Proof. Flat connections D.2 constructs the connected smooth universal cover \(p:\widetilde G\to G\). Choose \(\widetilde e\) over the identity. The product \(\widetilde G\times\widetilde G\) is simply connected: contract each coordinate of a loop separately. The map \((x,y)\mapsto p(x)p(y)\) therefore has a unique based lift \(\widetilde m:\widetilde G\times\widetilde G\to\widetilde G\), by the lifting theorem Flat connections D.3. Lift inversion in the same way. These lifts are smooth, since in every evenly covered neighbourhood they are compositions with a smooth local inverse of \(p\).

Both associativity maps on \(\widetilde G^3\) lift the same map into \(G\), and agree at the triple identity. Uniqueness of lifts on a connected domain makes them equal. The maps \(x\mapsto\widetilde m(\widetilde e,x)\) and \(x\mapsto\widetilde m(x,\widetilde e)\) likewise equal the identity. Multiplying \(x\) by its lifted inverse maps into the discrete fibre over the identity; it is constant because \(\widetilde G\) is connected, and its value at \(\widetilde e\) is \(\widetilde e\). These are the group axioms. Any other covering-group operations would be the same based lifts, proving uniqueness.

The local diffeomorphism \(p\) preserves brackets by differentiation, and its differential is an isomorphism. Its kernel is discrete by the covering charts. For a kernel element \(\gamma\), the continuous map \(x\mapsto x\gamma x^{-1}\) takes the connected group into that discrete kernel. It is constant and equals \(\gamma\) at the identity, proving centrality. □

Theorem F.2 (Negative Killing form and compact covers). If a connected Lie group has negative-definite Killing form, then the group and its simply connected covering group are compact. Their exponential maps are surjective.

Proof. Use the positive form \(-B\) at the identity. Killing invariance from Symmetric spaces H.5 makes every adjoint infinitesimal operator skew for this form. Exponentiation, followed by generation of the connected group by exponentials, proves invariance under the full adjoint action. Homogeneous spaces C.3 therefore gives a bi-invariant metric with complete Levi-Civita connection \[ \nabla_{X^L}Y^L=\tfrac12[X,Y]^L,\qquad R(X,Y)Z=-\tfrac14[[X,Y],Z]. \] Tracing \(Z\mapsto R(Z,X)Y\) gives \[ \operatorname{Ric}(X,Y)=-\tfrac14 B(X,Y)=\tfrac14 g(X,Y). \tag{F.1} \] The trace identity follows directly from \([[Z,X],Y]=\operatorname{ad}Y\,\operatorname{ad}X\,Z\).

If the dimension is zero the connected group is a point. Dimension one is impossible here: a one-dimensional Lie algebra is abelian and has zero Killing form. In dimension \(n\geq2\), Comparison D.1 applies to (F.1) with \(k=1/(4(n-1))\), proving compactness. Lemma F.1 constructs the universal covering group with the same Lie algebra. The identical metric and trace argument, or the covering part of Comparison D.1, proves it compact too.

For either group, Hopf–Rinow B.2 joins the identity to any prescribed point by a minimizing geodesic. The geodesics through the identity for the displayed connection are \(t\mapsto\exp(tX)\), by Homogeneous spaces C.3. Evaluating at time one proves surjectivity of the exponential. □

Theorem F.3 (Compact abelian groups and a dense cyclic subgroup). A connected compact abelian Lie group is a torus \[ \mathbb R^r/\Lambda \] for a full lattice \(\Lambda\). It contains an element whose integer powers are dense. A closed subgroup of a finite-dimensional Lie group is an embedded Lie subgroup, as proved in Invariant connections A.1.

Proof. The Lie algebra of an abelian group is abelian, since its invariant flows commute. Its exponential is a homomorphism from the additive Lie algebra: the two curves \(\exp(t(X+Y))\) and \(\exp(tX)\exp(tY)\) have the same initial value and invariant velocity, so agree by Local tools 2.3. The differential of the exponential at zero is the identity, obtained by differentiating \(t\mapsto\exp(tX)\). Local tools 1.2 makes it a local diffeomorphism. Its image is an open subgroup, hence all of the connected group. Its kernel \(\Lambda\) is discrete. The induced bijection \(\mathbb R^r/\Lambda\to G\) is a local diffeomorphism in quotient charts and hence a diffeomorphism and group isomorphism. Sectional curvature G.3 gives an integral basis of the discrete group and proves that compactness of this quotient is equivalent to full rank. A linear change of basis reduces to \(\Lambda=\mathbb Z^r\).

Choose real numbers \(\alpha_1,\ldots,\alpha_r\) such that \(1,\alpha_1,\ldots,\alpha_r\) are linearly independent over \(\mathbb Q\). Such a finite choice is possible recursively: the rational span of finitely many real numbers is countable, whereas \(\mathbb R\) is uncountable by the binary-sequence diagonal argument given in Symmetric spaces C.4. Let \(a\) be the class of \(\alpha=(\alpha_1,\ldots,\alpha_r)\) in the torus, and \(C\) the closure of its integer powers. Continuity of multiplication and inversion makes \(C\) a subgroup; it is closed, hence a compact embedded Lie subgroup by Invariant connections A.1.

Its identity component \(C^0\) is compact, connected and abelian, so the first paragraph applies to it. Write \(W\subseteq\mathbb R^r\) for its Lie algebra. The exponential of \(C^0\) is the restriction of the torus exponential, by uniqueness of the invariant ODE, so \[ C^0=\exp(W),\qquad C^0\simeq W/(W\cap\mathbb Z^r). \] Its compactness makes \(W\cap\mathbb Z^r\) span \(W\) by Sectional curvature G.3. If \(W\ne\mathbb R^r\), choose a nonzero integer vector \(m\) annihilating \(W\): an integral basis of this lattice gives a matrix of rank less than \(r\), whose homogeneous equations have a nonzero rational solution by Gaussian elimination; clearing denominators gives \(m\).

Then the torus character \(\chi_m([x])=e^{2\pi i m\cdot x}\) is one on \(C^0\). Components of the Lie group \(C\) are open, since an identity coordinate ball is connected and translates give neighbourhoods in each component. Compactness gives finitely many components, say \(N\). In the finite group \(C/C^0\), the order of each element is at most \(N\), by repetition among its first \(N+1\) powers, so its \(N!\)-th power is the identity. It follows that \(\chi_{N!m}\) is one on \(C\). In particular, \[ N!\,m\cdot\alpha\in\mathbb Z, \] contradicting the chosen rational independence. Therefore \(W=\mathbb R^r\). The subgroup \(C^0\), and hence \(C\), is the whole torus. This proves density. For \(r=0\) the torus is a point and its identity has the required property. □

G. Exercises with solutions

Exercise G.1 (Sphere, hyperboloid and scale). Give the compact and noncompact duality for the round sphere and real hyperbolic space, including the curvature normalization and the one-dimensional case.

Solution. For \(n\geq2\), the sphere presentation has algebra \(\mathfrak{so}(n+1)=\mathfrak{so}(n)\oplus\mathfrak m\), with \[ X(x)=\begin{pmatrix}0&-x^T\\x&0\end{pmatrix}, \qquad x\in\mathbb R^n. \] Its odd-odd bracket has bottom-right block \(-xy^T+yx^T\). In the hyperboloid presentation for the identity component of the group preserving \(\operatorname{diag}(-1,I_n)\), the odd matrices are \[ Y(x)=\begin{pmatrix}0&x^T\\x&0\end{pmatrix}, \] and their odd-odd bracket has bottom-right block \(xy^T-yx^T\). The even brackets and even actions on \(x\) agree. Thus these are exactly the dual brackets of D.3.

The sphere presentation is proved in Symmetric spaces E.1. For the future unit hyperboloid, the Lorentz boost \(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\cosh t&\sinh t\\\sinh t&\cosh t\end{smallmatrix}\right)\) on the plane spanned by the time axis and a spatial unit vector \(u\), extended by the identity on its orthogonal spatial complement, is a path from the identity preserving the form. It carries the base point to \((\cosh t,\sinh t\,u)\), and these points exhaust that sheet. Its stabilizer consists exactly of \(\operatorname{diag}(1,A)\), \(A\in SO(n)\); these rotations lie in the identity component by the plane-rotation argument in Affine transformations G.1. This proves the stated hyperboloid presentation as well.

With tangent inner product \(x\cdot y\), substitution in (0.1) gives \[ R_c(x,y)z=(y\cdot z)x-(x\cdot z)y,\qquad R_n(x,y)z=-R_c(x,y)z. \] For orthonormal \(x,y\) the sectional curvatures are respectively \(1\) and \(-1\). Multiplying the metric by \(r^2>0\) leaves its Levi-Civita connection unchanged, by the Koszul formula: both sides acquire the same factor. The curvature numerator acquires \(r^2\), and the two-plane squared-area denominator acquires \(r^4\), giving \(r^{-2}\) and \(-r^{-2}\). Sectional curvature C.1–C.3 proves that these are the radius-\(r\) round sphere and hyperboloid models, with their completeness and simple connectivity for \(n\geq2\).

When \(n=1\), the even algebra is zero and both displayed odd algebras are one-dimensional and abelian. Their curvature tensors vanish; there is no tangent two-plane. The global spaces are a circle and a line, and the algebraic duality leaves the same flat one-dimensional algebra. The compact/noncompact semisimple terminology of Part C therefore does not classify these one-dimensional models as a nonzero pair of opposite curvature types. □

Exercise G.2 (Which hypothesis removes invisible isotropy?). Prove the orthogonal decomposition, explain the Euclidean rotations, and determine what changes if effectiveness is replaced by \(\mathfrak h\cap Z(\mathfrak g)=0\).

Solution. For an effective pair, C.1 gives negative-definite even Killing form. Form the self-adjoint Killing operator on \(\mathfrak m\). The proof of C.2 shows that distinct eigenspaces commute, that the zero eigenspace is abelian and commutes with all odd vectors, and that every nonzero eigenspace generates a semisimple symmetric ideal. The signs of its eigenvalues distinguish compact and noncompact type. Decomposing each nonzero eigenspace into orthogonal invariant irreducible subspaces and repeating that bracket argument gives the irreducible blocks.

The even orthogonal complement of the brackets of all nonzero eigenspaces may still act on the zero eigenspace. It acts there faithfully and by skew operators, giving \(\mathfrak h_0\ltimes\mathfrak m_0\), not just a translation algebra. For example, take \[ \mathfrak g=\mathfrak{so}(n)\ltimes\mathbb R^n,\qquad \theta(A,x)=(A,-x). \] For \(n\geq2\) the standard skew action is faithful: if \(Ax=0\) for every \(x\), the matrix is zero. The positive form \(-\operatorname{tr}(AB)+x\cdot y\) is invariant under the even action, by cyclicity of trace and skew-adjointness. Odd brackets vanish, so this is an effective Euclidean pair retaining nonzero rotations. If instead the odd space is required to generate the whole algebra, C.2 proves that those rotations disappear.

Under the weaker central-intersection hypothesis, C.3 first splits off the kernel of the even action. It is a compact semisimple ideal lying entirely in \(\mathfrak h\); the remaining pair is effective, so the preceding construction applies. Without either hypothesis one can add a nonzero central even abelian summand acting trivially on everything. Its Killing form is zero, so it cannot be assigned to a compact semisimple ideal, and its invisible isotropy is not faithful Euclidean isotropy. This verifies why the hypothesis is needed for the stated decomposition. □

H. Maximal tori and connected centralizers

A torus in a Lie group is a compact connected abelian Lie subgroup. Part F identifies such a group with a real vector space modulo a full lattice. A maximal torus means one maximal by inclusion.

Lemma H.1 (Exponential surjectivity and containing tori). In a connected compact Lie group \(K\), every element is an exponential and belongs to a maximal torus. Every torus in \(K\) is contained in a maximal torus.

Proof. Symmetric spaces D.1 constructs a positive \(\operatorname{Ad}(K)\)-invariant inner product \(Q\) by integration. Its left-translated metric is bi-invariant, and Homogeneous spaces C.3 proves that its Levi-Civita connection is \[ \nabla_{X^L}Y^L=\tfrac12[X,Y]^L. \] That proof also shows that this connection is geodesically complete and that its geodesics from the identity are \(t\mapsto\exp(tX)\). Hopf–Rinow B.2 joins the identity to each \(k\in K\) by one of these geodesics. Thus \(k=\exp X\) for some \(X\in\mathfrak k\).

The closure of \(\{\exp(tX):t\in\mathbb R\}\) is a subgroup: continuity of multiplication and inversion shows that products and inverses of limits remain in the closure. It is abelian for the same reason. It is connected, since the closure of a connected set is connected, and compact as a closed subset of \(K\). To justify the connectedness fact, a separation of that closure into two nonempty relatively open sets would separate the original connected dense subset; density ensures that both sets meet it. The closed subgroup theorem, Invariant connections A.1, makes the closure an embedded Lie subgroup. It is therefore a torus containing \(k\).

Starting with any torus \(S\), choose among the tori containing it one of largest dimension. Such a choice uses only a maximum of a nonempty set of integers between zero and \(\dim K\). A proper inclusion of tori increases dimension: if two such tori have equal dimension, their Lie algebras agree, so uniqueness of the connected subgroup in Flat connections A.1 makes them equal. The chosen torus is thus maximal. Apply this also to the torus constructed from \(X\). The zero-dimensional connected group is the trivial group, for which all conclusions hold. □

Theorem H.2 (Conjugacy of maximal tori and central elements). All maximal tori of a connected compact Lie group are conjugate. Every central element belongs to every maximal torus.

Proof. Let \(T,S\subseteq K\) be maximal tori. By F.3 choose elements \(a\in T\), \(b\in S\) whose integer powers are dense in their respective tori. The exponential descriptions in F.3 give \(X\in\mathfrak t\), \(Y\in\mathfrak s\) with \[ a=\exp X,\qquad b=\exp Y. \] Use the invariant positive form \(Q\) from Symmetric spaces D.1. The smooth real function \[ f(k)=Q(\operatorname{Ad}(k)X,Y) \] attains a maximum at some \(k_0\), by compactness. Write \(U=\operatorname{Ad}(k_0)X\). Differentiating along \(\exp(tA)k_0\), for arbitrary \(A\in\mathfrak k\), gives \[ 0=Q([A,U],Y)=Q(A,[U,Y]). \tag{H.1} \] The derivative of the adjoint action is proved in Curvature and holonomy A.3. The second equality follows by differentiating invariance of \(Q\). Its nondegeneracy gives \([U,Y]=0\).

Commuting Lie algebra elements have commuting exponential curves. Indeed the adjoint curve satisfies \[ \frac{d}{dt}\operatorname{Ad}(\exp(tU))Y =[U,\operatorname{Ad}(\exp(tU))Y]; \] the constant value \(Y\) solves this equation, so uniqueness in Local tools 2.3 proves it is fixed. Naturality of the exponential, again from the invariant ODE, then proves the assertion. In particular \(k_0ak_0^{-1}\) commutes with \(b\).

Taking integer powers and then limits proves that every element of \(k_0Tk_0^{-1}\) commutes with every element of \(S\). Their product is an abelian subgroup. It is connected and compact, being the continuous image of the product of the two compact connected tori. Thus it is closed in the Hausdorff group and an embedded torus by Invariant connections A.1. Maximality forces \[ S=k_0Tk_0^{-1}. \]

Let \(z\in Z(K)\), and fix a maximal torus \(T\). By H.1 some maximal torus \(S\) contains \(z\). Choose \(k\) with \(kSk^{-1}=T\). Centrality gives \(kzk^{-1}=z\), so \(z\in T\). This proves the second assertion without assuming that maximal tori are already self-centralizing. □

Theorem H.3 (Connected centralizers). If \(S\) is a torus in a connected compact Lie group \(K\), then \[ C_K(S)=\{k\in K:ks=sk\text{ for every }s\in S\} \tag{H.2} \] is connected. More precisely it is the union of the maximal tori of \(K\) containing \(S\). For every \(Z\in\mathfrak k\), the group \[ C_K(Z)=\{k\in K:\operatorname{Ad}(k)Z=Z\} \tag{H.3} \] is connected, with Lie algebra \(\{X:[X,Z]=0\}\).

Proof. Fix \(c\in C_K(S)\). The centralizer of the single group element \(c\) is a closed subgroup, hence an embedded compact Lie subgroup by Invariant connections A.1. Let \[ L=C_K(c)^0 \] be its identity component. This component is closed: the closure of a connected subset is connected, so maximality of a component contains its closure. Thus \(L\) is a compact connected Lie group.

First \(c\) itself belongs to \(L\). By H.1 write \(c=\exp X\). The path \(\exp(tX)\), \(0\leq t\leq1\), commutes with \(c\) and joins the identity to \(c\). Also \(S\subseteq L\), because \(S\) is connected, contains the identity and commutes with \(c\).

By H.1 applied inside \(L\), choose a maximal torus \(T_L\subseteq L\) containing \(S\). The element \(c\) is central in \(L\) by its definition. Theorem H.2, also applied inside \(L\), therefore puts \(c\) in \(T_L\). Enlarge \(T_L\) to a maximal torus \(T\) of \(K\) using H.1. This torus contains both \(S\) and \(c\), and, being abelian, lies in \(C_K(S)\).

Conversely every torus containing \(S\) lies in its centralizer. The claimed union follows. Each torus in the union is connected and contains the identity; their union is connected. Indeed a separation of the union would separate one of these tori from the common identity, which is impossible. This proves (H.2).

For (H.3), let \(S_Z\) be the closure of \(\exp(\mathbb R Z)\). It is a torus by the closure argument in H.1. Exponential naturality shows that an element fixing \(Z\) centralizes this one-parameter subgroup and its closure. Conversely an element centralizing the closure fixes \(Z\) by differentiating at \(t=0\). Thus \[ C_K(Z)=C_K(S_Z), \] which is connected. Differentiating \(\operatorname{Ad}(\exp(tX))Z=Z\) proves the necessary Lie algebra condition. If \([X,Z]=0\), the adjoint ODE has constant solution \(Z\), so \(\exp(tX)\) lies in the centralizer for every \(t\); this proves sufficiency. The Lie algebra assertion follows from the closed subgroup characterization in Invariant connections A.1. □

I. The global compact Hermitian conclusion

Lemma I.1 (A simply connected group modulo connected isotropy). Let \(K\) be a connected simply connected Lie group and \(L\subseteq K\) a closed connected subgroup. Then \(K/L\) is simply connected.

Proof. Local tools 4.1 constructs the smooth quotient, its local sections and its principal-bundle product charts. It is connected as an image of \(K\), and it is path connected: a connected manifold is path connected by the coordinate-ball argument in A.2.

Let \(\alpha:[0,1]\to K/L\) be a continuous loop based at \(o=eL\). It has a continuous lift \(u:[0,1]\to K\) with \(u(0)=e\). Here only path lifting, not a covering-space assumption, is needed. To construct it, subdivide the interval into finitely many subintervals on each of which the image of \(\alpha\) lies in a domain of a local section. Such a subdivision exists by compactness: a finite open cover of the interval has a positive Lebesgue number. For completeness, if no positive number worked, intervals of lengths tending to zero failing to lie in any cover member would have centre points with a convergent subsequence; a neighbourhood of the limiting point lying in one cover member would eventually contain those whole intervals, a contradiction. On one subinterval, a section \(s\) gives a lift \(s(\alpha(t))h\), with constant \(h\in L\) uniquely chosen to equal the already assigned lift at its starting endpoint. This constructs the lift successively and continuously.

Its endpoint \(u(1)\) lies in \(L\). Since \(L\) is a connected manifold, join \(u(1)\) to \(e\) by a path \(v\) in \(L\). Concatenating \(u\) and \(v\) gives a loop in simply connected \(K\), and hence a based contraction. Project that contraction to \(K/L\). Its initial loop is \(\alpha\) followed by a constant segment at \(o\). Removing that segment by continuous reparametrization is a based homotopy to \(\alpha\). Thus every based loop contracts. □

Theorem I.2 (Compact Hermitian symmetric spaces are simply connected). A connected Hermitian symmetric space of compact type is compact and simply connected. No irreducibility assumption or connectedness assumption on its stabilizer is required.

Proof. Choose its connected effective symmetric isometry presentation \(G/H\), as in Symmetric spaces B.3. Compact type means that the Killing form of this semisimple pair is negative definite. Theorem F.2 therefore makes \(G\), and also its simply connected covering group \[ p:\widetilde G\longrightarrow G, \] compact. In particular \(G/H\) is compact as a continuous image of \(G\). Lemma F.1 identifies the two Lie algebras by \(dp_e\).

Theorem E.1 supplies \(Z\in\mathfrak z(\mathfrak h)\) with \[ \operatorname{ad}Z|_{\mathfrak m}=I,\qquad C_{\mathfrak g}(Z)=\mathfrak h,\qquad H\subseteq C_G(Z). \tag{I.1} \] The last inclusion holds for the full stabilizer, including all components. By H.3 the group \(C_G(Z)\) is connected and has Lie algebra \(\mathfrak h\). Its connected subgroup with this algebra is unique by Flat connections A.1. Consequently \(H^0=C_G(Z)\). Together with (I.1) this gives \[ H=C_G(Z), \tag{I.2} \] so connectedness of the stabilizer has been proved.

Let \(\widetilde Z=(dp_e)^{-1}Z\). Differentiation of \[ p(kgk^{-1})=p(k)p(g)p(k)^{-1} \] gives equivariance of \(dp_e\) under the two adjoint actions. Since this differential is injective, (I.2) implies the exact equality \[ p^{-1}(H)=C_{\widetilde G}(\widetilde Z). \tag{I.3} \] The group on the right is connected by H.3, applied to the compact connected group \(\widetilde G\). It is closed, being a stabilizer under a continuous linear action.

The map induced by \(p\) identifies \[ \widetilde G/p^{-1}(H)\ \longrightarrow\ G/H \tag{I.4} \] diffeomorphically. It is onto since \(p\) is onto, and equality of image cosets is exactly membership in \(p^{-1}(H)\), proving injectivity. In the quotient charts of Local tools 4.1 its differential is the induced isomorphism of tangent quotient spaces, because \(dp_e\) is an isomorphism; translate this at the identity coset to every point. Thus it is a local diffeomorphism by Local tools 1.2. Its bijectivity makes the local smooth inverses agree, proving the assertion.

Apply I.1 to the simply connected \(\widetilde G\) and the connected closed subgroup in (I.3). The quotient in (I.4), and hence the given space, is simply connected. The proof uses the single central generator of the full complex structure from E.1; it does not require a decomposition into irreducible spaces. For a zero-dimensional connected space the result says that a point is compact and simply connected. □

J. A covering-space exercise

Exercise J.1 (Prove connectedness before using it). Prove that every connected Hermitian symmetric space of compact type is simply connected, allowing a disconnected stabilizer at the outset. Identify where compactness of the universal covering group is needed. Explain why mere compactness of a Hermitian symmetric space would not suffice.

Solution. Begin with its effective connected presentation \(G/H\). Negative-definite Killing form gives the compact group and compact universal covering group by F.2; this is proved with the bi-invariant metric, its positive Ricci trace and Myers' theorem, not by assuming that covers of compact spaces are compact.

The central element \(Z\) in E.1 is fixed by every element of \(H\), and its Lie algebra centralizer is exactly \(\mathfrak h\). Theorem H.3 makes \(C_G(Z)\) connected, so equality of Lie algebras and the inclusions \(H^0\subseteq H\subseteq C_G(Z)\) force \(H=C_G(Z)\). On the universal covering group, adjoint equivariance gives \(p^{-1}(H)=C_{\widetilde G}(\widetilde Z)\). Compactness of \(\widetilde G\) is used to apply H.3 again, proving that this entire inverse image is connected. Lemma I.1 then contracts loops in its quotient by lifting a loop, closing the lift inside that subgroup, and projecting a contraction upstairs. This is the argument of I.2 with every connectedness step justified.

For the last question, let \[ M=\mathbb C/(\mathbb Z+i\mathbb Z). \] Translations preserve the Euclidean Hermitian metric and the complex coordinate structure, so they descend through the lattice quotient. Reflection about any \(p\in M\), namely \(z\mapsto2p-z\) modulo the lattice, is a well-defined holomorphic isometry fixing \(p\) with derivative \(-I\). The image of the closed unit square is all of \(M\), proving compactness.

The path \(t\mapsto[t]\), \(0\leq t\leq1\), is a loop whose lift to the plane starts at zero and ends at one. The quotient by the lattice is a covering: discs of radius less than \(1/3\) have disjoint lattice translates, giving its evenly covered charts. If the loop contracted, covering homotopy lifting, proved in Flat connections D.1, would keep the endpoint of its based lift fixed in the discrete fibre during that contraction. A constant loop has endpoint zero, a contradiction. Hence \(M\) is not simply connected. Its translation algebra is abelian, so its Killing form is zero; this compact example has Euclidean rather than compact semisimple type. □

K. Matrix square roots and unitary paths

For a complex matrix, \(X^*=\bar X^T\). A Hermitian matrix \(P=P^*\) is positive definite when \(v^*Pv>0\) for every nonzero complex column \(v\). On rectangular matrices put \(\|X\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2=\operatorname{tr}(X^*X)=\sum_{a,b}|X_{ab}|^2\).

Lemma K.1 (The matrix tools used below). A Hermitian matrix has an orthonormal complex eigenbasis and real eigenvalues. A positive-definite matrix has a unique positive-definite square root, which depends smoothly on the matrix. The groups \(U(n)\) and \(SU(n)\) are connected. For any rectangular \(Z\), the positive nonzero eigenvalues of \(Z^*Z\) and \(ZZ^*\), with multiplicities, agree. If \(I-Z^*Z>0\), then \(I-ZZ^*>0\).

Proof. Regard a Hermitian operator \(P\) as a self-adjoint real operator for the real inner product \(\operatorname{Re}(v^*w)\). The real spectral theorem, proved in De Rham decomposition H.2, gives an orthogonal decomposition into real eigenspaces with real eigenvalues. Each eigenspace is complex linear since \(P\) commutes with multiplication by \(i\). Inside each eigenspace choose a complex orthonormal basis by the following finite induction: normalize a nonzero vector, subtract its complex scalar projection from all remaining vectors, and repeat in its complex orthogonal complement. Real-orthogonal distinct complex eigenspaces are complex orthogonal, since their real orthogonality also holds after multiplying one vector by \(i\). This proves the first assertion.

For positive \(P\), all its eigenvalues \(\lambda\) are positive. Define \(P^{1/2}\) to be multiplication by \(\sqrt{\lambda}\) on each eigenspace. If \(R>0\) and \(R^2=P\), then \(R\) commutes with \(P\); on a \(P\)-eigenspace the eigenvalues of \(R\) are positive and have square \(\lambda\), so \(R=\sqrt{\lambda}I\). This proves uniqueness. To prove smoothness without choosing smooth eigenvectors, consider \(F(R)=R^2\) on Hermitian matrices. At \(R>0\), \[ dF_R(H)=RH+HR. \tag{K.1} \] In an orthonormal eigenbasis of \(R\), this multiplies the \(a,b\) entry of \(H\) by \(r_a+r_b>0\); it is an invertible real linear map of the Hermitian matrices. Positivity is open: \(v^*Rv\geq m|v|^2\) for the least positive eigenvalue \(m\), and a perturbation of operator norm less than \(m\) preserves positivity. Local tools 1.2 gives smooth local inverse maps for \(F\). Uniqueness makes them agree, proving global smoothness on the positive cone. The same proof on real symmetric matrices gives real symmetric square roots.

If \(Q\in U(n)\), write \(Q=A+iB\), where \[ A=\tfrac12(Q+Q^*),\qquad B=\tfrac1{2i}(Q-Q^*). \] These are commuting Hermitian operators. Diagonalize \(A\), then diagonalize the restriction of \(B\) to each \(A\)-eigenspace; commutation makes those restrictions legitimate. Thus \(Q=V\operatorname{diag}(e^{it_1},\ldots,e^{it_n})V^*\) with \(V\) unitary and real \(t_j\). The unit-circle parameterization is Connections E.1. Replacing each \(t_j\) by \(st_j\), \(0\leq s\leq1\), gives a path from \(I\) to \(Q\). If \(\det Q=1\), their sum is \(2\pi k\); replace one angle by \(t_j-2\pi k\), leaving \(Q\) unchanged and making the sum zero. The resulting path lies in \(SU(n)\).

Finally, for \(\lambda>0\), the map \(v\mapsto Zv\) identifies the \(\lambda\)-eigenspace of \(Z^*Z\) with that of \(ZZ^*\). Its inverse there is \(w\mapsto\lambda^{-1}Z^*w\). Both operators are positive semidefinite since \(v^*Z^*Zv=|Zv|^2\). Their nonzero eigenvalues and multiplicities therefore agree, and any remaining eigenvalues are zero. In particular, if all eigenvalues of \(Z^*Z\) are less than one, the same holds for \(ZZ^*\). This proves the last assertion. These eigenspace identities also give \[ Zf(Z^*Z)=f(ZZ^*)Z \tag{K.2} \] for any function \(f\) defined on the two finite spectra: on the zero eigenspaces both sides vanish, and on each nonzero eigenspace they agree. We will use this for positive square roots and inverse square roots. □

L. Complex Grassmannians, matrix balls and their curvature

Fix positive integers \(p,q\), and write \(\mathbb C^{p+q}=\mathbb C^p\oplus\mathbb C^q\). Matrices \(Z\) below have size \(q\times p\). A graph means the \(p\)-plane \[ E_Z=\{(v,Zv):v\in\mathbb C^p\}. \tag{L.1} \]

Theorem L.1 (The compact Grassmannian in graph coordinates). The space \(\mathrm{Gr}_p(\mathbb C^{p+q})\) of complex \(p\)-planes is a compact connected complex manifold \[ U(p+q)/(U(p)\times U(q)). \tag{L.2} \] It has a complete Hermitian symmetric metric whose expression in a graph chart is \[ \begin{aligned} P_+(Z)&=I_p+Z^*Z,\qquad Q_+(Z)=I_q+ZZ^*,\\ h_Z^+(U,V)&=\operatorname{tr}\bigl(P_+(Z)^{-1}U^*Q_+(Z)^{-1}V\bigr),\\ g_Z^+(U,V)&=\operatorname{Re}h_Z^+(U,V). \end{aligned} \tag{L.3} \] Its symmetry at \(E=\mathbb C^p\oplus0\) is \(Z\mapsto-Z\), and its symmetry at an arbitrary plane is the action on planes of the unitary reflection that is \(+I\) on that plane and \(-I\) on its orthogonal complement.

Proof. Extend an orthonormal basis of a \(p\)-plane to one of the whole space, using the finite orthogonal-complement induction in K.1. This proves transitivity of \(U(p+q)\); its stabilizer is exactly the block-diagonal subgroup \(U(p)\times U(q)\). The unitary group is closed and bounded in the real matrix space, hence compact by Local tools 0.1, and connected by K.1. Its polynomial equations make it a closed Lie subgroup by Invariant connections A.1. Local tools 4.1 supplies the smooth quotient in (L.2). It is compact and connected.

Planes whose projection onto the first summand is an isomorphism have the unique graph description (L.1). These are actual smooth quotient coordinates. For example a smooth unitary representative carrying \(E\) to \(E_Z\) is \[ k_Z= \begin{pmatrix} P_+^{-1/2}&-Z^*Q_+^{-1/2}\\ ZP_+^{-1/2}&Q_+^{-1/2} \end{pmatrix}. \tag{L.4} \] The columns have orthonormal blocks: their diagonal inner products are \(I_p,I_q\), and their cross inner product is zero. Thus \(k_Z^*k_Z=I\). Smoothness follows from K.1. Conversely, if a representative has first column blocks \(a,c\) with \(a\) invertible, its graph coordinate is \(ca^{-1}\), independent of the representative. Invertibility is open, so this chart and its inverse are smooth in the quotient charts. Every plane has such a chart after a unitary change of splitting.

For a fixed block matrix \(k=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\c&d\end{pmatrix}\), the coordinate action on its graph domain is \[ W=(c+dZ)(a+bZ)^{-1}. \tag{L.5} \] It is holomorphic: the entries of an inverse matrix are polynomial cofactors divided by its nonzero determinant. These transition maps give a complex atlas of dimension \(pq\), and the full unitary action is holomorphic.

Write \(L=a+bZ\) and \(T=d-Wb\). Differentiating (L.5) gives \[ dW(U)=TUL^{-1}. \tag{L.6} \] Preservation of the column inner product of \(\binom{I}{Z}\) gives \[ P_+(W)=L^{-*}P_+(Z)L^{-1}. \tag{L.7} \] The other required identity is \[ Q_+(W)=TQ_+(Z)T^*. \tag{L.8} \] To verify it, note \(TZ=Wa-c\), then expand \[ \begin{aligned} T(I+ZZ^*)T^* &=(d-Wb)(d^*-b^*W^*)+(Wa-c)(a^*W^*-c^*)\\ &=dd^*+cc^*+W(bb^*+aa^*)W^*\\ &\quad-(db^*+ca^*)W^*-W(bd^*+ac^*)\\ &=I+WW^*. \end{aligned} \] Here the block identities in the last step are \(kk^*=I\). In particular \(T\) is invertible. Substitute (L.6)–(L.8) into (L.3); adjacent inverse factors cancel, and cyclicity of trace leaves \(h_Z^+(U,V)\). Thus the formulas agree on overlaps and define an invariant smooth Hermitian form. Positivity is explicit: \[ h_Z^+(U,U)=\|Q_+^{-1/2}UP_+^{-1/2}\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2>0 \quad(U\ne0). \tag{L.9} \]

The unitary matrix \(\operatorname{diag}(I_p,-I_q)\) acts as \(Z\mapsto-Z\), hence fixes \(E\) and has derivative \(-I\) there. Its conjugate at another plane is precisely the stated reflection. All these maps are holomorphic isometries. Theorem A.2 makes the metric Kähler; Symmetric spaces A.2 proves completeness from these global point symmetries. This proves every metric and global assertion. □

Theorem L.2 (The noncompact matrix ball). Put \[ \mathcal D_{q,p}=\{Z\in M_{q,p}(\mathbb C):P_-(Z)=I_p-Z^*Z>0\}, \qquad Q_-(Z)=I_q-ZZ^*. \tag{L.10} \] This is a bounded convex complex domain. The group \[ U(p,q)=\{g:g^*\operatorname{diag}(I_p,-I_q)g =\operatorname{diag}(I_p,-I_q)\} \tag{L.11} \] acts transitively and holomorphically by (L.5), now defined for every \(Z\in\mathcal D_{q,p}\). The stabilizer of zero is \(U(p)\times U(q)\). The metric \[ h_Z^-(U,V)=\operatorname{tr}\bigl(P_-(Z)^{-1}U^*Q_-(Z)^{-1}V\bigr), \qquad g^-=\operatorname{Re}h^-, \tag{L.12} \] is invariant, Kähler, complete and Hermitian symmetric. The domain is simply connected, with symmetry \(Z\mapsto-Z\) at zero.

Proof. The condition in (L.10) is equivalent to \(\|Z\|_{\mathrm{op}}<1\), where \(\|Z\|_{\mathrm{op}}=\max_{|v|=1}|Zv|\); the maximum exists by compactness of the unit sphere and K.1 identifies its square with the largest eigenvalue of \(Z^*Z\). The triangle inequality follows by applying the vector triangle inequality to each unit vector. Consequently the unit ball is convex, bounded and open. Its straight-line contraction to zero proves simple connectivity. K.1 proves \(Q_->0\).

The graph \(E_Z\) is positive definite for the Hermitian form of signature \((p,q)\) exactly when \(P_->0\). If \(L=a+bZ\) had a nonzero kernel vector \(v\), the image under \(g\) of the positive-norm vector \((v,Zv)\) would have first component zero and hence nonpositive norm, a contradiction. Thus the denominator in (L.5) is invertible. Preservation of graph inner products gives \[ P_-(W)=L^{-*}P_-(Z)L^{-1}>0. \tag{L.13} \] The graph identity \(g\binom{I}{Z}=\binom{I}{W}L\) proves the action law by multiplication; the identity acts trivially, and inverses give inverse holomorphic transformations.

Here is an explicit element sending zero to \(Z\): \[ g_Z= \begin{pmatrix} P_-^{-1/2}&Z^*Q_-^{-1/2}\\ ZP_-^{-1/2}&Q_-^{-1/2} \end{pmatrix}. \tag{L.14} \] Its diagonal block inner products for the form (L.11) are \(I_p,-I_q\), and the off-diagonal block is zero, so it belongs to \(U(p,q)\). This proves transitivity. If \(g\) fixes zero, then \(c=0\). The identities \(g^*Jg=J\) give \(a^*a=I_p\), \(a^*b=0\), hence \(b=0\) and \(d^*d=I_q\). This proves the stabilizer assertion.

The group is a closed Lie subgroup by Invariant connections A.1. It is connected as well: \(s\mapsto g_{sZ}\) is a path from \(I\) to \(g_Z\), and every \(g\) is \(g_{g\cdot0}k\) for some \(k\in U(p)\times U(q)\), which is connected by K.1. The quotient identification is a diffeomorphism, since (L.14) is a smooth global section for its orbit map and Local tools 4.1 gives its quotient charts.

Equation (L.6) still holds. Its second metric identity is now \[ Q_-(W)=TQ_-(Z)T^*. \tag{L.15} \] Indeed \(TZ=Wa-c\), so expanding gives \[ \begin{aligned} T(I-ZZ^*)T^* &=dd^*-cc^*+W(bb^*-aa^*)W^*\\ &\quad+(-db^*+ca^*)W^*+W(-bd^*+ac^*)\\ &=I-WW^*. \end{aligned} \] The final step is \(gJg^*=J\), which follows from \(g^*Jg=J\) by inversion. Positivity of \(Q_-(W)\) implies invertibility of \(T\). Equations (L.6), (L.13) and (L.15) give invariance of (L.12) by the same explicit cancellation as before. Positivity is the squared norm in (L.9) with minus subscripts. Conjugating the isometry \(Z\mapsto-Z\) by the transitive holomorphic action gives a holomorphic point symmetry everywhere. Theorem A.2 proves the Kähler condition and Symmetric spaces A.2 proves completeness. □

Proposition L.3 (Opposite brackets and exact curvature scale). Under graph tangent coordinates at zero, the compact and noncompact metrics both have \(g_0(U,V)=\operatorname{Re}\operatorname{tr}(U^*V)\). Their curvature operators are \[ R_+(Z,W)V=ZW^*V-WZ^*V+VW^*Z-VZ^*W,\qquad R_-=-R_+. \tag{L.16} \] For a nonzero complex tangent vector \(Z\), their holomorphic sectional curvatures are \[ H_\pm(Z)= \frac{g_\pm(R_\pm(Z,iZ)iZ,Z)}{g_\pm(Z,Z)^2} =\pm4\, \frac{\operatorname{tr}((Z^*Z)^2)} {\operatorname{tr}(Z^*Z)^2}. \tag{L.17} \] In particular they lie respectively in \([4/m,4]\) and \([-4,-4/m]\), where \(m=\min(p,q)\).

Proof. Conjugation by \(\operatorname{diag}(I_p,-I_q)\) is an involution of either acting group, with fixed subgroup the block-diagonal stabilizer. The odd tangent matrices are respectively \[ X_+(Z)=\begin{pmatrix}0&-Z^*\\Z&0\end{pmatrix}, \qquad X_-(Z)=\begin{pmatrix}0&Z^*\\Z&0\end{pmatrix}. \tag{L.18} \] Differentiating the defining matrix-form equations gives these odd spaces and block-diagonal anti-Hermitian even parts. Conversely, any matrix satisfying the differentiated equation exponentiates into the group: the derivative of \((e^{tX})^*J e^{tX}\) is zero when \(X^*J+JX=0\), with \(J=I\) in the compact case and \(J=\operatorname{diag}(I_p,-I_q)\) in the other. The closed subgroup characterization in Invariant connections A.1 therefore proves equality of the stated Lie algebras. Differentiating the graph action at the identity sends either odd matrix to \(Z\). The odd brackets are opposite: \[ \begin{aligned} [X_+(Z),X_+(W)] &=\operatorname{diag}(W^*Z-Z^*W,\;WZ^*-ZW^*),\\ [X_-(Z),X_-(W)]&=-[X_+(Z),X_+(W)]. \end{aligned} \tag{L.19} \] The even action on the lower-left block is \(Z\mapsto dZ-Za\). The invariant metrics of L.1 and L.2 have the stated common value at zero, so Symmetric spaces B.2 identifies their Levi-Civita connections with the canonical ones and gives \(R=-[[\,\cdot,\cdot\,],\cdot]\). Applying this to (L.19) proves (L.16), including its sign.

This also realizes the algebraic duality of D.3 explicitly. In the complexified compact algebra, replace its odd space by \(i\mathfrak m\); conjugation by \(\operatorname{diag}(I_p,-iI_q)\) fixes the even space and takes \(iX_+(Z)\) to \(X_-(Z)\). Restricting to total trace zero gives the corresponding pair of special-unitary algebras, with the same odd brackets. The scalar even centre acts trivially on the quotient and has no effect on these tangent computations.

Putting \(W=iZ\) and \(V=iZ\) in (L.16) gives \(R_+(Z,iZ)iZ=4ZZ^*Z\). Also \(Z\) and \(iZ\) are real-orthogonal and have equal norm, so the denominator defining sectional curvature is exactly the one in (L.17). This proves that formula. If the nonzero eigenvalues of \(Z^*Z\) are \(\lambda_1,\ldots,\lambda_r>0\), then \(r\leq m\) by K.1 and \[ \frac1m\leq\frac1r \leq\frac{\sum_j\lambda_j^2}{(\sum_j\lambda_j)^2}\leq1. \] The upper bound follows by expanding the squared sum. The lower bound follows from \(r\sum_j\lambda_j^2-(\sum_j\lambda_j)^2=\sum_{j<k}(\lambda_j-\lambda_k)^2\geq0\). Rank-one matrices attain the upper ratio, and rectangular diagonal matrices with \(m\) equal positive singular values attain the lower ratio. Thus the claimed bounds have their stated exact constants. □

Example L.4 (The projective line and the disc). For \(p=q=1\), the compact chart and the noncompact domain have metrics \[ g_+=\frac{|dz|^2}{(1+|z|^2)^2}, \qquad g_-=\frac{|dz|^2}{(1-|z|^2)^2}, \tag{L.20} \] of constant Gaussian curvature \(4\) and \(-4\), respectively. The Cayley map \[ w=i\,\frac{1+z}{1-z} \tag{L.21} \] maps the unit disc biholomorphically onto the upper half-plane and pulls its metric \(|dw|^2/(\operatorname{Im}w)^2\) back to \(4g_-\), of curvature \(-1\).

Proof. Formula (L.20) is (L.3) and (L.12) for scalar matrices. In complex dimension one the real tangent plane is the plane spanned by \(Z,iZ\), so its Gaussian curvature is the holomorphic sectional curvature in (L.17), namely \(4\) or \(-4\). Compactness is that of the full projective line, not of its single graph chart.

For (L.21), direct subtraction of its conjugate gives \[ \operatorname{Im}w=\frac{1-|z|^2}{|1-z|^2}, \qquad dw=\frac{2i\,dz}{(1-z)^2}. \tag{L.22} \] Its inverse is \(z=(w-i)/(w+i)\); positivity of the imaginary part makes \(|w-i|<|w+i|\), proving that it lands in the disc. Both denominators are nonzero on their respective domains, so the two rational maps are inverse holomorphic maps. Substituting (L.22) proves the metric factor four. Multiplying a Riemannian metric by a positive constant leaves its Levi-Civita connection unchanged by the Koszul formula in Symmetric spaces A.1. Its sectional-curvature numerator scales by that constant and its denominator by its square; therefore curvature is divided by the constant. This gives curvature \(-1\) for \(4g_-\). □

M. The Siegel upper half-space and its bounded coordinates

For an integer \(r\geq1\), let \[ \mathcal H_r=\{W=X+iY: X,Y\in M_r(\mathbb R),\ X^T=X,\ Y^T=Y,\ Y>0\}. \tag{M.1} \] It is an open convex subset of the complex vector space of symmetric matrices, of complex dimension \(r(r+1)/2\). Positivity is open by K.1; it is convex because each quadratic form remains positive under convex combinations. Its tangent vectors are complex symmetric matrices.

Theorem M.1 (The symplectic action and the full stabilizer). With \[ \Omega=\begin{pmatrix}0&I_r\\-I_r&0\end{pmatrix}, \qquad \mathrm{Sp}(2r,\mathbb R)=\{g\in GL(2r,\mathbb R):g^T\Omega g=\Omega\}, \tag{M.2} \] the formula \[ g\cdot W=(AW+B)(CW+D)^{-1}, \qquad g=\begin{pmatrix}A&B\\C&D\end{pmatrix}, \tag{M.3} \] is a transitive holomorphic action on \(\mathcal H_r\). The group is connected, and the stabilizer of \(iI_r\) is \[ K=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}A&B\\-B&A\end{pmatrix}: A-iB\in U(r)\right\} \cong U(r). \tag{M.4} \] The resulting identification \(\mathrm{Sp}(2r,\mathbb R)/K\cong\mathcal H_r\) is a diffeomorphism.

Proof. Write \(F_W=\binom{W}{I_r}\). Since \(W^T=W\) and \(Y>0\), \[ F_W^T\Omega F_W=0,\qquad F_W^*\Omega F_W=-2iY. \tag{M.5} \] If \(N=CW+D\) had a nonzero kernel vector \(v\), then \(gF_Wv\) would have lower block zero and hence have zero pairing with itself under \(\Omega\). But the second identity in (M.5), and reality of \(g\), make that pairing \(-2i\,v^*Yv\ne0\). Thus \(N\) is invertible.

Set \(W'=(AW+B)N^{-1}\). The graph equation \[ gF_W=F_{W'}N \tag{M.6} \] and the first identity in (M.5) imply \(W'^T=W'\). The second identity then gives \[ Y'=\operatorname{Im}W'=N^{-*}YN^{-1}>0. \tag{M.7} \] So (M.3) maps the domain into itself. Equation (M.6), applied twice, proves the action law because matrix multiplication is associative and the normalized graph has unique upper block.
