Solution. Give the empty chain determinant one. Endpoint expansion of the simple-chain matrix \(T_l\) gives \(\det T_l=2\det T_{l-1}-\det T_{l-2}\). The initial values \(1,2\) imply \(\det T_l=l+1\) by induction. Positivity of these blocks is (O.6).

The square completion (O.7) is a triangular change of variables of determinant one. It splits off \(B\), so the original determinant is \(\det(B)(d-b^tB^{-1}b)\). For three arms, \[ c=\frac1{p+1}+\frac1{q+1}+\frac1{r+1}-1,\qquad \det Q=(p+1)(q+1)(r+1)c. \tag{V.2} \] The arm blocks remain positive, while the sign of \(c\) determines the remaining direction. Thus \[ \begin{array}{c|c|c|c} (p,q,r)&c&\det Q&\text{form}\\ \hline (1,2,4)&1/30&1&\text{positive}\\ (1,2,5)&0&0&\text{singular}\\ (1,2,6)&-1/42&-1&\text{indefinite}. \end{array} \] In the last case the arm directions are positive and the remaining direction negative, proving indefiniteness. For \((1,1,n-3)\), \(c=1/(n-2)\) and the determinant is \(2\cdot2\cdot(n-2)/(n-2)=4\), for every \(n\geq4\). At \(n=4\) the central coefficient is \(1/2\).

For the double-edge path, eliminate \(T_p\) to obtain \(T_q-\frac{2p}{p+1}e_1e_1^t\). Multilinearity in columns proves \(\det(I+uv^t)=1+v^tu\): terms using two updated columns vanish because those columns are proportional; the single-column terms sum to \(v^tu\). Factoring an invertible \(B\) therefore gives \(\det(B-tvv^t)=\det B(1-tv^tB^{-1}v)\). Using the inverse entry from (O.6) yields \[ \det Q=(p+1)(q+1)-2pq. \tag{V.3} \] The positivity test (O.9) is \(1-2pq/((p+1)(q+1))>0\). For the three requested pairs this expression is respectively \(1/9,0,-1/15\), and the determinants are \(1,0,-1\). These give the positive \(F_4\) case, its singular boundary, and an indefinite next case, since positive block directions remain in the last case. □

Exercise V.3 (row conventions and classification scope). Write \(B_3,C_3\) Cartan matrices and positive symmetrizers, explain why no vertex permutation identifies them, and state what O.2 classifies.

Solution. Number vertices along the chain with its double edge last. Then \[ \begin{aligned} A_B&=\begin{pmatrix}2&-1&0\\-1&2&-1\\0&-2&2\end{pmatrix}, &D_B&=\operatorname{diag}(2,2,1),\\ A_C&=\begin{pmatrix}2&-1&0\\-1&2&-2\\0&-1&2\end{pmatrix}, &D_C&=\operatorname{diag}(1,1,2). \end{aligned} \tag{V.4} \] Multiplication verifies symmetry of \(D_BA_B,D_CA_C\); positivity follows from (O.9) with sizes \(2,1\). The squared lengths are \(2d_i\), so the arrow points toward vertex \(3\) in \(B_3\) and toward vertex \(2\) in \(C_3\). The endpoint belonging to the double edge is intrinsic to the valued graph. Any isomorphism must preserve that endpoint and whether it is short; the two diagrams differ. In rank two, exchanging both vertices identifies the names \(B_2,C_2\).

O.2 classifies integral matrices with diagonal two, nonpositive off-diagonal entries, paired zeros and a positive diagonal symmetrization, up to simultaneous index permutation. Its connected table, or a multiset of connected blocks, determines exact entries. O.1 determines relative lengths within each component. Root systems are constructed in P.1–P.3 and Lie brackets in S.1–U.1; a diagram's name supplies neither construction without those proofs. Real forms and Hermitian pairs require further structure. □

W. Concrete rank-two roots and lattice checks

Exercise W.1 (six, eight and twelve roots). In the row convention realize \[ A_m=\begin{pmatrix}2&-1\\-m&2\end{pmatrix},\qquad S_m=\begin{pmatrix}2m&-m\\-m&2\end{pmatrix}, \qquad m=1,2,3, \tag{W.1} \] by explicit roots in a basis \(a,b\) with Gram matrix \(S_m\). Verify the root axioms and the arrow direction.

Solution. The first principal entry is positive and the completed-square remainder is positive because \(\det S_m=m(4-m)>0\). The simple reflections on coefficients are \[ s_a(x,y)=(-x+y,y),\qquad s_b(x,y)=(x,mx-y). \tag{W.2} \] Take each following set together with its negatives: \[ \begin{array}{c|l} m&R_m^+\\ \hline 1&a,\ b,\ a+b\\ 2&a,\ b,\ a+b,\ a+2b\\ 3&a,\ b,\ a+b,\ a+2b,\ a+3b,\ 2a+3b. \end{array} \tag{W.3} \] The complete stability check on positive members is below; linearity handles their negatives.

\(m\) root \(s_a\) image \(s_b\) image
1 \(a\) \(-a\) \(a+b\)
1 \(b\) \(a+b\) \(-b\)
1 \(a+b\) \(b\) \(a\)
2 \(a\) \(-a\) \(a+2b\)
2 \(b\) \(a+b\) \(-b\)
2 \(a+b\) \(b\) \(a+b\)
2 \(a+2b\) \(a+2b\) \(a\)
3 \(a\) \(-a\) \(a+3b\)
3 \(b\) \(a+b\) \(-b\)
3 \(a+b\) \(b\) \(a+2b\)
3 \(a+2b\) \(a+2b\) \(a+b\)
3 \(a+3b\) \(2a+3b\) \(a\)
3 \(2a+3b\) \(a+3b\) \(2a+3b\)

Every listed root is in the orbit of \(a\) or \(b\): \(a+b=s_ab\); for \(m=2\), \(a+2b=s_ba\); for \(m=3\), \(a+3b=s_ba\), \(a+2b=s_bs_ab\), and \(2a+3b=s_as_ba\). Negating a simple root is its own simple reflection, so the negative roots belong to these orbits too.

A product \(w\) of simple reflections is an isometry permuting the set. Reflection in \(wa\) or \(wb\) is \(ws_aw^{-1}\) or \(ws_bw^{-1}\), directly from (P.1). Hence reflection in every root preserves the set. If \(\alpha=wa\), then \[ \frac{2(\beta,\alpha)}{(\alpha,\alpha)} =\frac{2(w^{-1}\beta,a)}{(a,a)} \] is integral: \(w^{-1}\beta\) has integer coefficients and row one of \(A_m\) is integral. Row two handles the orbit of \(b\). The sets span because they contain \(a,b\). Inspection of (W.3) shows that no two distinct listed members are proportional, proving reducedness after adding negatives. The one-sign expansions make \(a,b\) a simple basis by P.1.

There are \(6,8,12\) roots, of types \(A_2,B_2,G_2\). In \(A_2\) every listed root has squared length two. In \(B_2\), \(a,a+2b\) have squared length four, and \(b,a+b\) squared length two. For \(m>1\), the arrow points toward \(b\), the shorter basis vector. In \(G_2\), substitution in \(S_3\) gives squared lengths \(2\) for \(b,a+b,a+2b\), and \(6\) for \(a,a+3b,2a+3b\). □

Exercise W.2 (integrality, primitive vectors and positivity). Explain why even lattice norms alone do not prove crystallographic integrality for long roots, why integral reflection orbits of basis vectors are primitive and reduced, and how height descent acts on the highest \(B_2\) root. Identify the failure at off-diagonal entries \(-2,-2\).

Solution. In the integral lattice with Gram matrix \[ \begin{pmatrix}4&-1\\-1&2\end{pmatrix}, \] every norm \(4x^2-2xy+2y^2\) is even for integral \(x,y\). The form is positive, by completing a square and using determinant \(7\). Yet for its basis vectors \(u,v\), \(2(u,v)/(u,u)=-1/2\). Norm two would remove that denominator; longer vectors require further divisibility. In W.1 it comes from the integral Cartan row and remains true under the integral isometries (W.2).

An integral invertible matrix with integral inverse preserves primitive vectors, meaning those whose coordinates have greatest common divisor one. If \(d>1\) divided all transformed coordinates, the integral inverse would make it divide all original coordinates. Every simple reflection is its own integral inverse, and every basis vector is primitive.

If primitive integral vectors satisfy \(y=cx\), a nonzero coordinate makes \(c=p/q\) rational, with coprime integers and \(q>0\). Integrality of \(y\) implies \(q\) divides every coordinate of \(x\), so \(q=1\). Then primitiveness of \(y\) gives \(|p|=1\). Thus \(y=\pm x\), proving reducedness of any set made from such orbits. This does not assert that every primitive lattice vector is an orbit root. Finiteness alone would be insufficient: \(\{\pm a,\pm2a\}\) is finite and nonreduced.

In \(B_2\), \(\gamma=a+2b\) has coroot pairing \(-2+4=2\) with \(b^\vee\), so \[ s_b\gamma=\gamma-2b=a. \tag{W.4} \] Its height drops from three to one. The other nonsimple positive root \(a+b\) has pairing one with \(a^\vee\), and \(s_a(a+b)=b\), lowering height from two to one. In both cases a positive coefficient outside the reflected position remains, preventing the reflected root from being negative. The general positive-norm and one-sign arguments are T.3.

For the matrix \[ \begin{pmatrix}2&-2\\-2&2\end{pmatrix}, \tag{W.5} \] the candidate Gram form has null vector \((1,1)\). Its formal coefficient reflections are \(s_1(x,y)=(-x+2y,y)\), \(s_2(x,y)=(x,2x-y)\), with \[ s_1s_2=I+N,\qquad N=\begin{pmatrix}2&-2\\2&-2\end{pmatrix},\qquad N^2=0. \] Induction gives \((I+N)^n=I+nN\) for \(n\geq0\), so the orbit of \((1,0)\) contains every \((1+2n,2n)\), an infinite set. The null vector \((1,1)\) is fixed by both reflections. Positive squared norm can no longer force a positive root pairing. Also invertibility no longer identifies different degrees with different Cartan weights as in T.1. These failures explain the positivity hypothesis; none of the finite-type conclusions applies to (W.5). □

Exercise W.3 (long-root sublattices and mixed signs). Find the stronger norm divisibility for long-root orbits in \(B_2,G_2\). In the coordinate model of \(C_4\), show that a mixed-sign vector can have long-root norm while still failing to be a root, and explain the additional invariant that excludes it.

Solution. In W.1 with \(m=2\) or \(3\), let \[ M_m=\{(x,y)\in\mathbb Z^2:y\in m\mathbb Z\}. \] Both transformations (W.2) preserve it: \(s_a\) keeps \(y\), and \(s_b\) replaces it by \(mx-y\). It contains \(a=(1,0)\), so it contains the long-root orbit. On writing \(y=mz\), its squared norm is \[ 2m x^2-2m xy+2y^2 =2m(x^2-mxz+mz^2). \tag{W.6} \] Positive norms on this sublattice are therefore at least \(2m\), not merely two. The long roots in W.1 have precisely norm \(2m\).

Here is how this stronger bound excludes mixed signs. For any coefficient vector \(c\), write \(c=p-q\), where \(p,q\) have nonnegative integer coordinates with disjoint supports. The off-diagonal Gram entries are nonpositive, so \((p,q)\leq0\). Consequently \[ \|p-q\|^2\geq\|p\|^2+\|q\|^2. \tag{W.7} \] If \(c\in M_m\), both \(p,q\) are in \(M_m\). If both are nonzero, (W.6) and positivity give norm at least \(4m\), exceeding a long root's \(2m\). For the ordinary integer lattice, norms in all three matrices (W.1) are positive even integers; (W.7) gives the weaker bound four. That suffices for every norm-two root, including all \(A_2\) roots, but cannot by itself exclude all long-root norms.

For the precise \(C_4\) example use the complete coordinate construction in De Rham decomposition AE.3: \[ \alpha_i=e_i-e_{i+1}\ (1\leq i\leq3),\qquad \alpha_4=2e_4, \] with orthonormal \(e_i\). Its Gram matrix has diagonal \(2,2,2,4\) and adjacent entries \(-1,-1,-2\). The vector \[ x=\alpha_1-\alpha_3=e_1-e_2-e_3+e_4 \] has squared norm four and mixed simple coefficients. It is not a long root, since the actual long roots in the proved coordinate model are \(\pm2e_i\).

The relevant sublattice in simple coefficients is \[ M=\{(c_1,c_2,c_3,c_4)\in\mathbb Z^4: c_1,c_2,c_3\text{ are even}\}. \tag{W.8} \] Indeed the orthonormal coordinates of \(\sum c_i\alpha_i\) are \[ (c_1,\ -c_1+c_2,\ -c_2+c_3,\ -c_3+2c_4). \] They are all even exactly when \(c\in M\): one implication is immediate, and the reverse follows by successive addition of the first three coordinates. Thus \(M\) is exactly \(2\mathbb Z^4\) in orthonormal coordinates. All its positive norms are multiples of four. The simple reflections are adjacent coordinate swaps and a sign change of the fourth coordinate, as verified in the same earlier proof; they preserve \(2\mathbb Z^4\). Hence the orbit of the long simple vector lies in \(M\), and \(x\) fails its parity condition.

Taking positive and negative parts of simple coefficients preserves \(M\). Applying (W.7) to a mixed-sign member of \(M\) gives norm at least eight, excluding every long root of norm four. This is the additional bound that ordinary evenness alone cannot give. □

X. Changing a simple basis explicitly

Exercise X.1 (finite maximization in \(A_2\)). In W.1 with \(m=1\), let \(v=-a\). Find its positive simple basis, a reflection word carrying it to \(\{a,b\}\), and an actual score-maximizing orbit vector for the argument of P.2.

Solution. The pairings of \(v\) with \(a,b,a+b\) are \(-2,1,-1\). Thus \(v\) is regular, and its positive roots are \(b,-a,-a-b\). Their indecomposable members are \[ \Delta_v=\{b,-a-b\}, \] since their sum is \(-a\). The other two cannot be sums of roots from this three-member positive set, as their coefficients show.

Put \(\eta=a+b\); its pairings with \(a,b\) are both one. By W.1 the orbit of \(v\) is the six roots. Their scores against \(\eta\) are \[ \begin{array}{c|rrrrrr} x&a&b&a+b&-a&-b&-a-b\\ \hline (x,\eta)&1&1&2&-1&-1&-2. \end{array} \] The unique maximum is \(a+b\). The word \(w=s_bs_a\), with its rightmost factor acting first, sends \[ v=-a\ \longmapsto\ a\ \longmapsto\ a+b. \] On the positive basis it sends \(b\mapsto a+b\mapsto a\) and \(-a-b\mapsto-b\mapsto b\). Thus \(w\Delta_v=\{a,b\}\), realizing both the maximum and the basis conjugacy of P.2. □

Exercise X.2 (a \(B_2\) basis change and a singular vector). Use \(S_2\) in W.1. In coefficient coordinates let \(\eta=S_2^{-1}(1,1)^t\) and \(v=s_a\eta\). Determine its simple basis and arrow. Then use \(v_0=-a\) to test whether a maximizing orbit vector must have strictly positive simple pairings without regularity.

Solution. Solving gives \(\eta=(1,3/2)\), and (W.2) gives \(v=(1/2,3/2)\). Its pairings with \(a,b\) are \((-1,2)\), hence with \(a+b,a+2b\) they are \(1,3\). All are nonzero. Its positive roots are \[ -a,\quad b,\quad a+b,\quad a+2b, \] with indecomposable basis \(a'=-a,\ b'=a+b\). Indeed \(b=a'+b'\) and \(a+2b=a'+2b'\), and a unit coefficient vector cannot be a sum of two nonzero nonnegative coefficient vectors. These basis vectors are \(s_aa,s_ab\), so their Gram matrix is again \(S_2\) and their row Cartan matrix is again \(\begin{pmatrix}2&-1\\-2&2\end{pmatrix}\), of type \(B_2\). Their squared lengths are \(4,2\); the arrow still points to the short vector \(b'\).

For \(v_0=-a\), the pairings with \(a,b\) are \((-4,2)\), and \((v_0,a+2b)=0\). Thus it is singular. Its orbit is the four long roots \(\pm a,\pm(a+2b)\), by the reflection table. Their scores against \(\eta\) are \(1,-1,3,-3\), respectively. The maximum \(a+2b\) has simple pairings \((0,2)\), not two strictly positive values. This exhibits exactly the missing strictness in the proof of P.2 when regularity is dropped. □

Exercise X.3 (the disconnected rank-two system and its scales). Explain the usual notation \(D_2\) and the length-scale freedom that its Cartan matrix cannot detect.

Solution. In an orthonormal coordinate plane the roots \(\{\pm e_1\pm e_2\}\) are \[ \{\pm\beta,\pm\gamma\},\qquad \beta=e_1-e_2,\quad\gamma=e_1+e_2. \] The two displayed vectors are perpendicular, each of squared length two. Their reflections change the sign in their own line and fix the other, so this is \(A_1\) orthogonal to \(A_1\), with Cartan matrix \(\operatorname{diag}(2,2)\). Thus it lies outside the connected \(D_n\), \(n\geq4\), range in O.2.

Replace \(\beta,\gamma\) independently by \(s\beta,t\gamma\), with \(s,t>0\). Orthogonality, reflection closure and Cartan matrix stay unchanged; the squared lengths become \(2s^2,2t^2\). Varying \(s/t\) changes their relative length. A single global scale would preserve that ratio, up to interchanging the two lines, so it cannot account for all these possibilities. This is the componentwise scale freedom proved in O.1 and P.1. □

Y. Matrix algebras, roots and the Killing normalization

Write \(E_{ij}\) for the matrix with a single entry one in position \(i,j\). Matrix multiplication gives \[ E_{ij}E_{kl}=\delta_{jk}E_{il},\qquad [E_{ij},E_{kl}]=\delta_{jk}E_{il}-\delta_{li}E_{kj}. \tag{Y.1} \]

Exercise Y.1 (the \(A_2\) algebra and inverse Killing metrics). Realize the \(A_2\) presentation by traceless \(3\times3\) matrices and prove that the map is an isomorphism. Compute the Killing and inverse root Gram matrices in ranks one and two, including their positive real Cartan spaces.

Solution. Put \[ \begin{gathered} e_1=E_{12},\quad e_2=E_{23},\quad f_1=E_{21},\quad f_2=E_{32},\\ h_1=\operatorname{diag}(1,-1,0),\qquad h_2=\operatorname{diag}(0,1,-1). \end{gathered} \tag{Y.2} \] For diagonal \(H\), (Y.1) gives \([H,E_{ij}]=(H_{ii}-H_{jj})E_{ij}\). This proves the \(h,e\) and \(h,f\) relations for the type \(A_2\) matrix \(\begin{pmatrix}2&-1\\-1&2\end{pmatrix}\). The \(h_i\) commute; (Y.1) gives \([e_i,f_j]=\delta_{ij}h_i\). Also \[ [e_1,e_2]=E_{13},\qquad [f_1,f_2]=-E_{31}. \] Both \(e_1,e_2\) commute with \(E_{13}\), and both \(f_1,f_2\) commute with \(E_{31}\), again by (Y.1). These are all the Serre relations. Hence the presentation maps to \(\mathfrak{sl}_3(\mathbb C)\).

Its image contains all six off-diagonal matrix units and the two independent diagonal vectors \(h_1,h_2\). These form a basis of the traceless matrices: an arbitrary diagonal trace-zero vector is a unique combination of the latter two, independently of the six off-diagonal entries. Thus the map is surjective onto a space of dimension eight. W.1 gives six \(A_2\) roots, and T.3 gives presentation dimension \(2+6=8\); surjectivity and equality of finite dimensions prove the isomorphism. The same argument in rank one sends \(e,f,h\) to \(E_{12},E_{21},\operatorname{diag}(1,-1)\) and gives the three-dimensional algebra \(\mathfrak{sl}_2\).

Here is a direct trace computation of the Killing form. On all \(n\times n\) matrices let \(L_X(Z)=XZ\), \(R_Y(Z)=ZY\). Using the basis \(E_{ij}\), the diagonal coefficient in \(L_XR_Y(E_{ij})\) is \(X_{ii}Y_{jj}\). Hence \[ \begin{gathered} \operatorname{tr}_{\mathrm{End}}(L_XR_Y) =\operatorname{tr}X\,\operatorname{tr}Y,\\ \operatorname{tr}_{\mathrm{End}}(L_XL_Y) =n\operatorname{tr}(XY),\qquad \operatorname{tr}_{\mathrm{End}}(R_XR_Y) =n\operatorname{tr}(YX). \end{gathered} \] The last two follow by applying the same matrix-unit calculation to left or right multiplication by \(XY\) or \(YX\). Since \(\operatorname{ad}X=L_X-R_X\), expansion gives \(2n\operatorname{tr}(XY)-2\operatorname{tr}X\operatorname{tr}Y\). For traceless \(X,Y\), the decomposition of all matrices as \(\mathfrak{sl}_n\oplus\mathbb CI\) is invariant under both adjoints, and their action on \(\mathbb CI\) is zero. Their trace on the traceless summand is therefore \[ K(X,Y)=2n\operatorname{tr}(XY). \tag{Y.3} \]

In rank one, \(K(h,h)=8\) and the root satisfies \(\alpha(h)=2\). Its Killing-dual vector is \(T_\alpha=h/4\), so \[ (\alpha,\alpha)=K(T_\alpha,T_\alpha)=\frac12, \qquad h_\alpha=\frac{2T_\alpha}{(\alpha,\alpha)}=h. \tag{Y.4} \] The real line \(\mathbb Rh\) is positive, while \(K(ih,ih)=-8\). The imaginary line is therefore not the positive real Cartan space of Q.5.

For (Y.2), put \(C=\begin{pmatrix}2&-1\\-1&2\end{pmatrix}\). The Cartan Gram matrix and its inverse are \[ K_{\mathfrak h}=6C= \begin{pmatrix}12&-6\\-6&12\end{pmatrix}, \qquad K_{\mathfrak h}^{-1}=\frac1{18} \begin{pmatrix}2&1\\1&2\end{pmatrix}. \tag{Y.5} \] The simple-root covectors have coordinate rows \((2,-1)\) and \((-1,2)\), so their Gram matrix in the inverse Killing metric is \[ C K_{\mathfrak h}^{-1}C^t =\frac16 C =\begin{pmatrix}1/3&-1/6\\-1/6&1/3\end{pmatrix}. \tag{Y.6} \] This is one sixth of the common abstract \(A_2\) normalization \(C\). In particular \(T_{\alpha_i}=h_i/6\), and the normalized coroot remains \(h_i\). On \(\mathbb Rh_1+\mathbb Rh_2\), the form is \(6\) times the sum of the squares of the real diagonal entries, positive except at zero. It is a real form of the complex Cartan plane. This distinguishes its intrinsic Killing scale from freely chosen abstract root-length units. □

Exercise Y.2 (strings, parity and proportional roots). Compute the actual \(\alpha\)-string through \(\beta\) for \(\alpha=\varepsilon_1-\varepsilon_2\), \(\beta=\varepsilon_2-\varepsilon_3\) in (Y.2). Check the endpoint sign for a short root of squared length two and a long root of squared length six with inner product \(-3\). Explain the even-weight zero-multiplicity argument and the additional step excluding nonintegral proportional roots.

Solution. The root vectors for \(\beta,\beta+\alpha\) are \(E_{23},E_{13}\). Put \(E=\operatorname{ad}E_{12}\), \(F=\operatorname{ad}E_{21}\), \(H=\operatorname{ad}h_1\). Formula (Y.1) gives \[ \begin{array}{c|cc} &E_{23}&E_{13}\\ \hline H&-E_{23}&E_{13}\\ E&E_{13}&0\\ F&0&E_{23}. \end{array} \tag{Y.7} \] The roots of the matrix Cartan are exactly the six \(\varepsilon_i-\varepsilon_j\), because the six off-diagonal units have those weights and the diagonal subspace has weight zero. Thus the string is exactly \(\beta,\beta+\alpha\), with lower and upper parameters \(r=0,q=1\). The formula \(r-q=\beta(h_\alpha)\) gives \(-1\), and \(s_\alpha\beta=\beta+\alpha\).

For the other calculation, take \(\alpha=b,\beta=a\) in the \(G_2\) system W.1. The entire string is \(\beta,\beta+\alpha,\beta+2\alpha,\beta+3\alpha\). Its coroot pairing is \(2(-3)/2=-3\), so \(r-q=0-3=-3\) and \(s_\alpha\beta=\beta+3\alpha\). These scalar checks concern the verified abstract root system. The existence of a Lie algebra carrying it is supplied separately by T.1–U.1; a list of four root vectors in a Euclidean plane does not by itself define brackets.

By Q.1, an irreducible finite-dimensional \(\mathfrak{sl}_2\) module \(V_m\) has weights \(m,m-2,\ldots,-m\), each once. A completely reducible module having only even weights can contain only even \(m\). Every such summand, including \(V_0\), contributes one zero weight. If the zero space has dimension one and a \(V_2\) summand is present, that is the entire module. Merely knowing that the zero space has dimension one, without the given \(V_2\), would also permit a single \(V_4\) or another even highest weight.

Finally, suppose \(\delta=c\alpha\) is a proportional root and root-space theory has excluded integral multiples of magnitude at least two for every root. The actual coroot triples and weight integrality in Q.3 give \[ 2c\in\mathbb Z,\qquad 2/c\in\mathbb Z. \] The first relation already makes \(c\) real and rational. The two nonzero integers have product four, so \(c\in\{\pm1/2,\pm1,\pm2\}\). The values \(\pm2\) are excluded by the hypothesis for \(\alpha\); the values \(\pm1/2\) are excluded by the same hypothesis for \(\delta\), since then \(\alpha=\pm2\delta\). Thus only opposite or equal roots remain. Excluding integral multiples in one chosen root direction without this reciprocal argument would not establish reducedness. □

Exercise Y.3 (the solvable and zero cases). For \([H,X]=X\) in a two-dimensional algebra, compute Cartan weights and the Killing form and explain the semisimplicity hypothesis. State the output for the zero algebra.

Solution. In the ordered basis \(H,X\), the adjoint matrices are \[ \operatorname{ad}H=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\0&1\end{pmatrix}, \qquad \operatorname{ad}X=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\-1&0\end{pmatrix}. \] They give Killing matrix \(\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\0&0\end{pmatrix}\). Relative to \(\mathbb CH\), the weights are zero on \(H\) and \(\alpha(H)=1\) on \(X\); the negative weight is absent. The derived algebra is \(\mathbb CX\), whose own bracket is zero, so the whole algebra is solvable and its Killing form degenerate. Its one nonzero weight cannot be a root system: reflection in it would require its negative. This verifies concretely why Q.2–Q.5 require semisimplicity.

For the zero algebra, the Cartan, its positive real form and its dual are all zero, and the root set and diagram are empty. Root production alone gives these data; the converse construction and uniqueness for all diagrams require S.1–U.1. The empty presentation there indeed has no generators and is the zero algebra by S.1. Neither conclusion is a classification of real forms or Hermitian pairs. □

Z. Calculations inside the presentation

Exercise Z.1 (vacuum values, an overlap and the Serre exponent). In (S.7), calculate \(E_iF_i^k1\) and explain the use of variable vacuum values. Resolve both reductions of \(ehf\) in the ordered-word theorem for \(f<h<e\). For the long-first \(G_2\) Cartan matrix, identify the two positive Serre powers and the vanishing coefficient in (T.4).

Solution. For \(k\geq1\), commute \(E_i\) past the \(k\) copies of \(F_i\), using \([E_i,F_i]=H_i\) and \(E_i1=0\). The \(H_i\) at a suffix of length \(j\) acts there by \(t_i-2j\), by (S.7). Thus \[ E_iF_i^k1 =\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}(t_i-2j)F_i^{k-1}1 =k(t_i-k+1)F_i^{k-1}1. \tag{Z.1} \] At \(k=0\) the result is zero. If \(\sum c_ih_i\) were zero, its action on the polynomial vacuum would give the zero polynomial \(\sum c_it_i\), forcing every coefficient to vanish. One fixed evaluation \(t_i=\lambda_i\) would give only the scalar equation \(\sum c_i\lambda_i=0\); in general that cannot distinguish all coefficient vectors. The variable vacuum, or all evaluations together, proves the required independence.

The relations for sorting the three rank-one generators are \[ eh=he-2e,\qquad hf=fh-2f,\qquad ef=fe+h. \] Sorting the left inverted pair first gives \[ \begin{aligned} ehf&=(he-2e)f\\ &=h(fe+h)-2(fe+h)\\ &=(fh-2f)e+h^2-2fe-2h\\ &=fhe+h^2-4fe-2h. \end{aligned} \] Sorting the right inverted pair first gives \[ \begin{aligned} ehf&=e(fh-2f)\\ &=(fe+h)h-2(fe+h)\\ &=f(he-2e)+h^2-2fe-2h\\ &=fhe+h^2-4fe-2h. \end{aligned} \tag{Z.2} \] All terms in the final expression are ordered. This is the explicit rank-one instance of the general Jacobi overlap in S.1.

For \(A=\begin{pmatrix}2&-1\\-3&2\end{pmatrix}\), the positive relations are \[ (\operatorname{ad}e_1)^2e_2=0,\qquad (\operatorname{ad}e_2)^4e_1=0. \tag{Z.3} \] The short-root row is the second, so it carries the fourth power. The coefficient \(-m(a_{ij}+m-1)\) in (T.4) is respectively \(-2(-1+2-1)=0\) and \(-4(-3+4-1)=0\). Interchanging these exponents would not give the prescribed row convention. The negative relations and their coefficients follow from the signed involution (S.8). □

Exercise Z.2 (degrees and the sign of a reflected vector). Explain the different reasons that type \(A_2\) has no degree \(a-b\) or \(2a\) in the presentation. For \(\gamma=a+b\), compute its reflection in \(a\) and the corresponding action on \(e_{12}=[e_1,e_2]\) in (Y.2).

Solution. The degree \(a-b\) has both signs, so the exact triangular decomposition T.1 excludes it. The degree \(2a\) is in the positive cone, but its positive bracketed words use only \(e_1\). Their length is at least two, so they vanish by \([e_1,e_1]=0\) and induction on bracketed words, as proved in T.1. The two exclusions use different parts of that proof.

The root \(\gamma=a+b\) has \(\gamma(h_1)=2-1=1\), hence \(s_a\gamma=b\); its height drops from two to one. In (Y.2), \(e_{12}=E_{13}\). Put \(E=\operatorname{ad}E_{12}\), \(F=\operatorname{ad}E_{21}\). Formula (Y.1) gives \(EE_{13}=0\), \(FE_{13}=E_{23}\), \(FE_{23}=0\), and \(EE_{23}=E_{13}\). Applying (T.5) from right to left therefore gives \[ E_{13} \ \xmapsto{\exp E}\ E_{13} \ \xmapsto{\exp(-F)}\ E_{13}-E_{23} \ \xmapsto{\exp E}\ -E_{23}. \tag{Z.4} \] Thus the reflected space is the \(b\)-space, but this particular normalized vector acquires a minus sign. Root reflection and the scalar choice of a root vector are different data. □

Exercise Z.3 (disconnected and exceptional existence, rescaling and the boundary). Reconstruct \(\operatorname{diag}(2,2)\), identify the nonvanishing argument for the \(E_8\) presentation, and show that rescaling simple root vectors does not change the presented algebra. Include the empty case and the failure of the finite-type proof for (W.5).

Solution. Each rank-one presentation has basis \(e,f,h\) and is \(\mathfrak{sl}_2\) by Y.1. For \(\operatorname{diag}(2,2)\), every cross-component generator bracket is zero: the positive and negative Serre exponents are one, while all other cross brackets vanish in (S.5). The two commuting inclusions and projections in T.4 therefore identify the presentation exactly with \[ \mathfrak{sl}_2\oplus\mathfrak{sl}_2, \] of dimension six. Its four roots are the two opposite pairs in X.3. This differs from the connected type \(A_2\) algebra of dimension eight and six roots in Y.1.

For \(E_8\), the positive matrix is provided by O.2, and a root system with that precise diagram is proved in De Rham decomposition AE.4 and transferred to the row convention in P.1. Its 240 roots comprise \(4\binom82=112\) two-coordinate vectors and \(2^7=128\) half-coordinate vectors with the proved parity condition. The actual Lie-algebra existence argument is the following chain of proved constructions. S.1 constructs the formal free Lie algebra and proves its tensor embedding. The module of S.2 keeps all Cartan generators independent. T.1 identifies the exact Serre ideal and shows that every simple generator survives. T.2 realizes root reflections by automorphisms. T.3 then forces exactly the finite root set, with one-dimensional root spaces, and T.4 proves simplicity. Consequently the \(E_8\) algebra exists and has dimension \(8+240=248\). Counting an abstract orbit alone would prove none of the presentation's noncollapse or Jacobi-compatible bracket assertions.

For arbitrary nonzero \(c_i\in\mathbb C\), the substitutions \[ e_i\longmapsto c_i e_i,\qquad f_i\longmapsto c_i^{-1}f_i,\qquad h_i\longmapsto h_i \tag{Z.5} \] preserve (S.5). In particular the factors cancel in \([e_i,f_i]=h_i\); off-diagonal mixed brackets remain zero. A positive Serre expression of exponent \(m\) is multiplied by \(c_i^m c_j\), and a negative one by \(c_i^{-m}c_j^{-1}\), so both remain zero. The universal property gives an endomorphism, and substitution of the reciprocal scalars gives its inverse. It is therefore an automorphism. This accounts for arbitrary choices of nonzero simple root vectors in the one-dimensional spaces; normalization of the opposite vector to keep \(h_i\) fixes its reciprocal factor.

For rank zero the free presentation is zero, with empty root system and no components. For (W.5), the determinant and positive definiteness required in T.1 and T.3 fail; W.2 exhibits the null vector and an infinite formal reflection orbit explicitly. The identification of degrees with distinct weights and the positive-norm height argument cannot be used there. The proof consequently makes no finite-type or semisimplicity assertion for that boundary matrix. □

AA. Compact real forms and real semisimple algebras

A conjugation of a complex Lie algebra \(\mathfrak g\) is an antilinear bracket-preserving map \(\rho\) with \(\rho^2=1\). Its fixed algebra \(\mathfrak g^\rho\) is a real form: every \(X\in\mathfrak g\) has the unique expression \[ X=\frac{X+\rho X}{2} +i\,\frac{X-\rho X}{2i} \tag{AA.1} \] with both displayed summands before multiplication by \(i\) fixed by \(\rho\). Conversely a real form determines a conjugation by \(U+iV\mapsto U-iV\). We call a real form compact if its real Killing form is negative definite. This terminology agrees with compact groups by F.2. In this part \(\mathfrak g\) is a finite-dimensional complex semisimple algebra, and \(K\) is its complex Killing form.

Theorem AA.1 (construction of a compact real form). In the Serre generators of U.1, the assignments \[ \tau h_i=-h_i,\qquad \tau e_i=-f_i,\qquad \tau f_i=-e_i \tag{AA.2} \] extend antilinearly to a conjugation. Its fixed algebra \(\mathfrak u\) is a compact real form. The form \[ B(X,Y)=-K(X,\tau Y) \tag{AA.3} \] is a positive-definite Hermitian form, linear in its first argument. In particular, every complex semisimple algebra has a compact real form.

Proof. U.1 identifies \(\mathfrak g\) with the algebra defined by the real-coefficient Serre relations. Conjugating coefficients of formal words therefore gives an antilinear involution \(\kappa\) fixing every generator. The interchange \(\omega\) of (S.8) preserves the positive and negative Serre relations as well as the preliminary relations: each positive iterated bracket goes to its negative counterpart with the same exponent. Thus it descends to this quotient. Compose \(\kappa\), \(\omega\), and the scaling \(e_i\mapsto-e_i,\ f_i\mapsto-f_i,\ h_i\mapsto h_i\) from Z.3. This gives (AA.2), preserves the bracket, and has square one on the generators, hence on the whole algebra. Formula (AA.1) proves the real-form assertion.

For any conjugation \(\eta\), use a real basis of its fixed algebra as a complex basis of \(\mathfrak g\). The matrix of \(\operatorname{ad}(\eta X)\) is the entrywise conjugate of that of \(\operatorname{ad}X\). Taking traces gives \[ K(\eta X,\eta Y)=\overline{K(X,Y)}. \tag{AA.4} \] Symmetry of \(K\) and (AA.4) show that \(B\) is Hermitian. Killing invariance gives, without yet assuming positivity, \[ B([Z,X],Y)=B(X,[-\tau Z,Y]). \tag{AA.5} \] Indeed \(K([Z,X],\tau Y)=K(X,[\tau Y,Z])\), and applying \(\tau\) to \([-\tau Z,Y]\) gives \([\tau Y,Z]\).

Let \(\mathfrak h\) be the Cartan spanned by the \(h_i\), and let \(\mathfrak h_{\mathbb R}\) be their real span. By Q.5, \(K\) is positive definite on this real span. Since \(\tau=-1\) there, (AA.3) is its positive Hermitian extension to \(\mathfrak h\). The operators \(\operatorname{ad}h_i\) are self-adjoint for \(B\) by (AA.5), and all their root eigenvalues are real by Q.5. The simultaneous weight spaces with different weights are \(B\)-orthogonal: if \(\lambda\ne\mu\) are their eigenvalues for some \(h_i\), then \((\lambda-\mu)B(X,Y)=0\). The roots are determined by their values on the \(h_i\), so this applies to distinct root spaces and to a root space paired with \(\mathfrak h\).

On a simple root line, \[ B(e_i,e_i)=K(e_i,f_i)=\tfrac12K(h_i,h_i)>0. \tag{AA.6} \] The equality follows from \(K([e_i,f_i],h_i)=K(e_i,[f_i,h_i])=2K(e_i,f_i)\). We now prove positivity on every positive root line by height induction. For a nonsimple positive root \(\beta\), the height argument used in U.1 supplies a simple root \(\alpha_i\) such that \(\gamma=\beta-\alpha_i\) is a positive root. These two roots are not proportional to \(\alpha_i\), since the system is reduced. By Q.4, the \(\alpha_i\)-string through \(\gamma\) is an irreducible rank-one module. Put \(E=\operatorname{ad}e_i\), \(F=\operatorname{ad}f_i\). For a nonzero \(v\in\mathfrak g_\gamma\), the root \(\beta\) exists, so \(Ev\ne0\). In the convention (Q.3), \(v\) is a multiple of some \(v_j\) with \(1\leq j\leq m\), and \[ FEv=j(m-j+1)v. \] By (AA.5), \(E\) and \(F\) are adjoint to one another. Consequently \[ B(Ev,Ev)=B(v,FEv) =j(m-j+1)B(v,v)>0. \tag{AA.7} \] Here the scalar is real and positive, and the induction hypothesis applies to the smaller height of \(\gamma\). Root spaces have dimension one by Q.3, so this proves their positivity.

For \(T\in\mathfrak h_{\mathbb R}\) and \(X\in\mathfrak g_\alpha\), applying \(\tau\) to \([T,X]=\alpha(T)X\) shows that \(\tau X\in\mathfrak g_{-\alpha}\). Also \[ B(\tau X,\tau X)=-K(\tau X,X)=B(X,X). \] Thus the negative root lines are positive as well. Their orthogonal direct sum with \(\mathfrak h\) is all of \(\mathfrak g\), proving positive definiteness of \(B\).

On \(\mathfrak u=\mathfrak g^\tau\), \(B=-K\). The real Killing form of \(\mathfrak u\) is exactly \(K|_{\mathfrak u}\), because the real adjoint matrices in a real basis also compute the complex trace after complexification. It is therefore negative definite. Symmetric spaces H.5–H.6 give semisimplicity of \(\mathfrak u\). The construction in C.4 realizes it as the Lie algebra of \(\operatorname{Aut}(\mathfrak u)^0\): the bracket equations define a closed matrix subgroup, and its derivation algebra is \(\operatorname{ad}\mathfrak u\simeq\mathfrak u\). Now F.2 makes this group and its simply connected cover compact. For \(\mathfrak g=0\), all assertions hold on the zero vector space and the group is a point. □

Lemma AA.2 (powers of a positive automorphism). Suppose \(A\) is a complex Lie-algebra automorphism which is positive and self-adjoint for a positive-definite Hermitian form. For every real \(t\), the spectral power \(A^t\) is an automorphism, and \(D=\log A\) is a derivation. If the algebra is semisimple, then \[ D=\operatorname{ad}Z,\qquad A^t=\exp(t\operatorname{ad}Z) \tag{AA.8} \] for a unique \(Z\). If an antilinear involution \(\eta\) satisfies \(\eta A\eta=A^{-1}\), then \(\eta A^t\eta=A^{-t}\). If it commutes with \(A\), it commutes with \(A^t\) and \(D\).

Proof. K.1 supplies an orthogonal direct sum of eigenspaces \(V_\lambda\) with \(\lambda>0\). Bracket preservation gives \[ [V_\lambda,V_\mu]\subseteq V_{\lambda\mu}, \tag{AA.9} \] where the bracket is zero if \(\lambda\mu\) is absent from the spectrum. Define \(A^t\) and \(D\) on \(V_\lambda\) by the real scalars \(\lambda^t\) and \(\log\lambda\). Since \((\lambda\mu)^t=\lambda^t\mu^t\), (AA.9) proves that \(A^t\) preserves the bracket; \(A^{-t}\) is its inverse. Since \(\log(\lambda\mu)=\log\lambda+\log\mu\), the same calculation proves the derivation rule for \(D\). Symmetric spaces H.6 makes this derivation uniquely inner, and the exponential on each eigenspace proves (AA.8).

If \(\eta A\eta=A^{-1}\), then \(\eta\) carries \(V_\lambda\) to \(V_{\lambda^{-1}}\). The eigenvalues are real, so its antilinearity does not change them; the power identity follows on each eigenspace. If \(\eta\) commutes with \(A\), it preserves every \(V_\lambda\), and commutes with every indicated real scalar operator there. This also covers the zero algebra. □

Theorem AA.3 (conjugacy of compact real forms). Any two compact conjugations \(\tau_0,\tau_1\) of \(\mathfrak g\) are conjugate by an automorphism of the form \(\exp(\operatorname{ad}Z)\). If both commute with another conjugation \(\rho\), the conjugating automorphism can be chosen to preserve \(\mathfrak g^\rho\), with \(Z\in\mathfrak g^\rho\).

Proof. For a compact conjugation \(\tau_i\), put \(B_i(X,Y)=-K(X,\tau_iY)\). If \(X=U+iV\) with \(U,V\in\mathfrak g^{\tau_i}\), symmetry and reality of \(K\) give \[ B_i(X,X)=-K(U,U)-K(V,V). \tag{AA.10} \] Thus \(B_i\) is positive definite, as well as Hermitian by (AA.4). Let \(A=\tau_0\tau_1\), a complex automorphism. Then \[ B_1(X,Y)=B_0(X,AY). \tag{AA.11} \] Hermitian symmetry on both sides makes \(A\) self-adjoint for \(B_0\), and (AA.10) makes it positive. Also \[ \tau_0 A\tau_0=A^{-1},\qquad \tau_1=A^{-1}\tau_0. \tag{AA.12} \] Apply AA.2. The automorphism \(\phi=A^{-1/2}\) satisfies \[ \phi\tau_0\phi^{-1} =A^{-1/2}\tau_0A^{1/2} =A^{-1}\tau_0=\tau_1. \tag{AA.13} \] It is the exponential of the inner derivation \(-\tfrac12\log A\), which proves the first assertion.

If \(\rho\) commutes with both conjugations, it commutes with \(A\) and, by AA.2, with \(\log A\) and \(\phi\). Write \(\log A=\operatorname{ad}W\). Conjugating this equality by \(\rho\) gives \(\operatorname{ad}(\rho W)=\operatorname{ad}W\). The zero centre in Symmetric spaces H.6 gives \(\rho W=W\). Hence \(Z=-W/2\) lies in the real fixed algebra, and \(\phi\) preserves that algebra. □

Theorem AA.4 (a compact conjugation commuting with any real form). For every conjugation \(\rho\) of \(\mathfrak g\), there is a compact conjugation \(\tau\) commuting with \(\rho\). Writing \(\mathfrak u=\mathfrak g^\tau\) and \(\sigma=(\rho\tau)|_{\mathfrak u}\), one has an involution of the compact real algebra and a direct sum \[ \mathfrak g^\rho=\mathfrak u_+\oplus i\mathfrak u_-, \qquad \mathfrak u_\pm=\ker(\sigma\mp1). \tag{AA.14} \] The real Killing form is negative on \(\mathfrak u_+\), positive on \(i\mathfrak u_-\), and makes them orthogonal.

Proof. Choose \(\tau_0\) by AA.1 and put \(\tau_1=\rho\tau_0\rho\). Its fixed real algebra is \(\rho(\mathfrak g^{\tau_0})\). Formula (AA.4) shows that \(K\) is again real and negative definite there, so \(\tau_1\) is compact. As in AA.3, \(A=\tau_0\tau_1\) is positive self-adjoint for \(B_0\). In addition to (AA.12), it satisfies \[ \rho A\rho=\tau_1\tau_0=A^{-1}. \tag{AA.15} \] Define \[ \tau=A^{-1/2}\tau_0 =A^{-1/4}\tau_0A^{1/4}. \tag{AA.16} \] The second identity follows from AA.2. It exhibits \(\tau\) as an automorphic conjugate of \(\tau_0\), hence as a compact conjugation. Using (AA.15) and \(\rho\tau_0\rho=\tau_1=A^{-1}\tau_0\), one obtains \[ \rho\tau\rho=A^{1/2}\tau_1 =A^{-1/2}\tau_0=\tau. \tag{AA.17} \] This proves commutation.

The complex-linear map \(\rho\tau\) now has square one and preserves \(\mathfrak u\); its restriction \(\sigma\) is a real Lie-algebra involution. For \(U,V\in\mathfrak u\), the equation \(\rho(U+iV)=U+iV\) is precisely \(\sigma U=U,\ \sigma V=-V\). This proves (AA.14). An automorphism preserves \(K\), because it conjugates each adjoint operator. Consequently the two eigenspaces of \(\sigma\) are \(K\)-orthogonal. Negativity on \(\mathfrak u\) and \(K(iV,iV)=-K(V,V)\) prove the signs.

On the real algebra \(\mathfrak g^\rho\), the restriction \(\theta=\tau|_{\mathfrak g^\rho}\) is an involution with these positive and negative eigenspaces. The form \(-K(X,\theta Y)\) is positive definite: for \(X=U+iV\) as above its squared value is \(-K(U,U)-K(V,V)\). Thus \(\theta\) is a Cartan involution, meaning an involution with this positivity property. All bracket inclusions follow directly from multiplying its eigenvalue signs. Semisimplicity of the real algebra follows from Symmetric spaces H.6, since its complexification is \(\mathfrak g\). □

Theorem AA.5 (real forms are classified by compact involutions). Fix a compact real form \(\mathfrak u\) of \(\mathfrak g\). Sending an involution \(\sigma\in\operatorname{Aut}_{\mathbb R}(\mathfrak u)\) to \[ \mathfrak r_\sigma=\mathfrak u_+\oplus i\mathfrak u_- \tag{AA.18} \] induces a bijection between conjugacy classes of involutions under \(\operatorname{Aut}_{\mathbb R}(\mathfrak u)\) and isomorphism classes of real forms of \(\mathfrak g\). The Killing signature of \(\mathfrak r_\sigma\), in the order (positive, negative), is \[ (\dim_{\mathbb R}\mathfrak u_-,\,\dim_{\mathbb R}\mathfrak u_+). \tag{AA.19} \]

Proof. Extend \(\sigma\) complex linearly to \(\mathfrak g\), and let \(\tau\) be its fixed compact conjugation. The extension commutes with \(\tau\), since both preserve the decomposition \(\mathfrak u+i\mathfrak u\). Thus \(\rho_\sigma=\sigma_{\mathbb C}\tau\) is a conjugation, whose fixed algebra is (AA.18). Its bracket is closed: even-even and odd-odd brackets are even, and even-odd brackets are odd; the two factors of \(i\) in an odd-odd bracket only change its sign. The orthogonality and signs in (AA.19) follow exactly as in AA.4. Compactly conjugate involutions clearly give isomorphic real forms by the complex extension of the conjugating map.

Conversely, AA.4 supplies for any real conjugation \(\rho\) a commuting compact conjugation \(\tau'\). By AA.3, choose a complex automorphism carrying \(\tau'\) to \(\tau\). It carries \(\rho\) to a conjugation commuting with \(\tau\). Their product then restricts to an involution of \(\mathfrak u\), and its real form is the image of \(\mathfrak g^\rho\). This proves surjectivity.

For injectivity, suppose \(f:\mathfrak r_{\sigma_1}\to\mathfrak r_{\sigma_2}\) is a real Lie-algebra isomorphism. By (AA.1), its complex-linear extension \(\Phi\) is an automorphism of \(\mathfrak g\) satisfying \(\Phi\rho_{\sigma_1}\Phi^{-1}=\rho_{\sigma_2}\). The compact conjugation \(\tau'=\Phi\tau\Phi^{-1}\) commutes with \(\rho_{\sigma_2}\), as does \(\tau\). The relative assertion of AA.3 therefore supplies an automorphism \(\psi\), commuting with \(\rho_{\sigma_2}\), such that \(\psi\tau'\psi^{-1}=\tau\). Now \(F=\psi\Phi\) preserves \(\tau\), hence preserves \(\mathfrak u\), and it still intertwines the two \(\rho_{\sigma_i}\). Multiplying by \(\tau\) shows that \(F|_{\mathfrak u}\) conjugates \(\sigma_1\) to \(\sigma_2\). This proves injectivity, including semisimple algebras with several simple factors and the zero algebra. □

AB. Involutions and invariant simple roots

Let \(\mathfrak u\) be a compact real semisimple algebra, let \(\mathfrak g=\mathfrak u\otimes_{\mathbb R}\mathbb C\), and let \(\tau\) be its conjugation. Write \(K\) for the complex Killing form. For an involution \(\sigma\) of \(\mathfrak u\), use the same letter for its complex extension and put \[ \mathfrak k=\mathfrak u_+,\qquad \mathfrak p=\mathfrak u_-,\qquad Q=-K|_{\mathfrak u}. \tag{AB.1} \]

Theorem AB.1 (a Cartan adapted to the fixed algebra). The fixed algebra is reductive: \[ \mathfrak k=Z(\mathfrak k)\oplus[\mathfrak k,\mathfrak k], \tag{AB.2} \] where the second summand is compact semisimple. There is a \(\sigma\)-stable maximal abelian subalgebra \(\mathfrak t=\mathfrak t_+\oplus\mathfrak t_-\) of \(\mathfrak u\), with \(\mathfrak t_\pm\subseteq\mathfrak u_\pm\), such that \(\mathfrak t_+\) is maximal abelian in \(\mathfrak k\). Its complexification \(\mathfrak h=\mathfrak t_{\mathbb C}\) is a Cartan subalgebra of \(\mathfrak g\). No root of \((\mathfrak g,\mathfrak h)\) vanishes identically on \(\mathfrak t_+\). One can consequently choose a positive system, and hence a simple-root basis, preserved by \(\sigma\).

Proof. Invariance of the positive form \(Q\) gives \[ [\mathfrak k,\mathfrak k]^{\perp_Q}=Z(\mathfrak k). \tag{AB.3} \] Indeed \(Q(X,[Y,Z])=Q([X,Y],Z)\); its vanishing for all \(Y,Z\in\mathfrak k\) is equivalent to \([X,Y]=0\) for every \(Y\), by positive definiteness on \(\mathfrak k\). Taking orthogonal complements proves (AB.2). Write \(\mathfrak s=[\mathfrak k,\mathfrak k]\). Its centre is zero: an element centralizing \(\mathfrak s\) within \(\mathfrak s\) also centralizes \(Z(\mathfrak k)\), hence belongs to \(Z(\mathfrak k)\cap\mathfrak s=0\). On \(\mathfrak s\), each \(\operatorname{ad}X\) is skew-adjoint for \(Q\). In an orthonormal real basis, \[ K_{\mathfrak s}(X,X) =\operatorname{tr}_{\mathfrak s}(\operatorname{ad}X)^2 =-\sum_j\lVert[X,v_j]\rVert_Q^2. \tag{AB.4} \] This is strictly negative for \(X\ne0\), since the centre of \(\mathfrak s\) is zero. Symmetric spaces H.5 proves semisimplicity. This proves the first assertion without assuming a theorem about reductive fixed subalgebras.

Choose an abelian subalgebra \(\mathfrak t_+\) of maximal dimension in \(\mathfrak k\). Then \(C_{\mathfrak k}(\mathfrak t_+)=\mathfrak t_+\), since any commuting element could otherwise be adjoined. Choose a subspace \(\mathfrak t_-\subseteq\mathfrak p\) of maximal dimension whose elements commute with one another and with \(\mathfrak t_+\). The direct sum \(\mathfrak t=\mathfrak t_+\oplus\mathfrak t_-\) is abelian and \(\sigma\)-stable. Its centralizer in \(\mathfrak u\) is \(\sigma\)-stable and therefore splits into its two eigenspaces. Its even part lies in \(C_{\mathfrak k}(\mathfrak t_+)=\mathfrak t_+\); an odd element commuting with \(\mathfrak t\) can be adjoined to \(\mathfrak t_-\), so maximality places it in \(\mathfrak t_-\). Thus \(C_{\mathfrak u}(\mathfrak t)=\mathfrak t\).

For \(T\in\mathfrak t\), \(\operatorname{ad}T\) is skew-adjoint for \(Q\); on the complexification it is skew-adjoint for the positive Hermitian form \(B=-K(\,\cdot\,,\tau\,\cdot\,)\) from (AA.10). Hence \(i\operatorname{ad}T\) is self-adjoint. K.1 diagonalizes it. A finite basis of the commuting operators can be simultaneously diagonalized by successively restricting to their invariant eigenspaces. It follows that \(\mathfrak h=\mathfrak t_{\mathbb C}\) is toral, and complexifying the centralizer equation gives \(C_{\mathfrak g}(\mathfrak h)=\mathfrak h\). This is the Cartan definition of Q.2. Its roots take purely imaginary values on \(\mathfrak t\).

The action on roots is \((\sigma\alpha)(H)=\alpha(\sigma^{-1}H)\). If a root \(\alpha\) vanished on \(\mathfrak t_+\), this equation on \(\mathfrak t_+\oplus\mathfrak t_-\) would give \(\sigma\alpha=-\alpha\). For \(0\ne e\in\mathfrak g_\alpha\), the vector \(e+\sigma e\) is nonzero because its two terms lie in distinct root spaces. It is fixed by \(\sigma\) and commutes with \(\mathfrak t_+\). But \[ C_{\mathfrak k_{\mathbb C}}(\mathfrak t_+) =(\mathfrak t_+)_{\mathbb C}, \] by taking real and imaginary parts in the earlier centralizer equation. This is a subspace of \(\mathfrak h\), whereas \(e+\sigma e\) has only nonzero root components, a contradiction.

There are only finitely many roots. Since their restrictions are nonzero, choose \(T\in\mathfrak t_+\) on none of their kernels; the finite-hyperplane argument is proved in De Rham decomposition AD.5. Then the real numbers \(-i\alpha(T)\) are all nonzero. Declare a root positive when this number is positive. Q.5 identifies the real span of the roots with a Euclidean root space, and P.1 proves that such a regular real linear functional defines a positive system with a simple basis. Since \(\sigma T=T\), this functional has the same value on \(\alpha\) and \(\sigma\alpha\). Thus \(\sigma\) preserves positive roots and their indecomposable members, the simple roots. The zero algebra has empty roots and requires no choice of \(T\). □

Theorem AB.2 (signed diagram representatives). Every involution of a compact semisimple real algebra admits Serre generators in its complexification for which \[ \begin{aligned} \tau h_i&=-h_i,&\tau e_i&=-f_i,&\tau f_i&=-e_i,\\ \sigma h_i&=h_{\pi(i)},& \sigma e_i&=c_i e_{\pi(i)},& \sigma f_i&=c_i^{-1}f_{\pi(i)}. \end{aligned} \tag{AB.5} \] Here \(\pi^2=1\) is a permutation preserving the row Cartan matrix, \(c_i=c_{\pi(i)}=1\) on every two-element orbit, and \(c_i\in\{1,-1\}\) on fixed vertices. Conversely each such diagram permutation and choice of signs defines an involution of the compact form constructed in AA.1. Every compact-involution conjugacy class has at least one of these representatives; distinct choices are not asserted to give distinct classes.

Proof. Use the Cartan and simple roots of AB.1. Let \(T_\alpha\in\mathfrak h\) be the \(K\)-dual root vector, defined by \(K(T_\alpha,H)=\alpha(H)\). Since roots are purely imaginary on \(\mathfrak t\), writing \(T_\alpha=U+iV\) with \(U,V\in\mathfrak t\) gives \(K(U,T)=0\) for every \(T\in\mathfrak t\). Negativity on \(\mathfrak t\) gives \(U=0\), so \(T_\alpha\in i\mathfrak t\), where \(K\) is positive definite. In particular \[ q_\alpha=K(T_\alpha,T_\alpha)>0,\qquad h_\alpha=2T_\alpha/q_\alpha. \tag{AB.6} \] These are the same coroots as in Q.3–Q.5.

The conjugation \(\tau\) carries \(\mathfrak g_\alpha\) to \(\mathfrak g_{-\alpha}\): for \(T\in\mathfrak t\), conjugating the purely imaginary eigenvalue reverses its sign. Choose \(0\ne e\in\mathfrak g_{\alpha_i}\) and put \(f=-\tau e\). The opposite-root calculation of Q.3 gives \[ [e,f]=K(e,f)T_{\alpha_i}=B(e,e)T_{\alpha_i}. \] Multiplying \(e\) by a positive real scalar \(a\) and \(f\) by the same scalar changes the coefficient to \(a^2B(e,e)\). Choose \(a^2=2/(q_{\alpha_i}B(e,e))\). Then \([e_i,f_i]=h_i=h_{\alpha_i}\) and \(\tau e_i=-f_i\). The generators so obtained satisfy the Serre relations and generate \(\mathfrak g\), by the proof of U.1.

By AB.1, \(\sigma\) permutes the simple roots by an involutive permutation \(\pi\). Invariance of \(K\) gives \(\sigma T_{\alpha_i}=T_{\alpha_{\pi(i)}}\), hence \(\sigma h_i=h_{\pi(i)}\). It also proves \[ \alpha_j(h_i)=\alpha_{\pi(j)}(h_{\pi(i)}), \] so \(\pi\) preserves the row Cartan matrix. One-dimensional root spaces give nonzero scalars \(c_i\) with \(\sigma e_i=c_i e_{\pi(i)}\). The bracket relation forces \(\sigma f_i=c_i^{-1}f_{\pi(i)}\). Since \(\sigma\) commutes with \(\tau\), it is unitary for \(B\). The normalization \[ B(e_i,e_i)=\tfrac12K(h_i,h_i) =\tfrac12K(h_{\pi(i)},h_{\pi(i)}) =B(e_{\pi(i)},e_{\pi(i)}) \] therefore gives \(|c_i|=1\). Also \(\sigma^2=1\) gives \(c_i c_{\pi(i)}=1\); at fixed vertices this means \(c_i=\pm1\).

For each two-element orbit \(i,j=\pi(i)\), replace \[ e_i\ \text{by }e_i,\quad e_j\ \text{by }c_i e_j, \qquad f_i\ \text{by }f_i,\quad f_j\ \text{by }c_i^{-1}f_j. \tag{AB.7} \] These are the inverse scalings of Z.3, so preserve all presentation relations. Since \(|c_i|=1\), they also preserve \(\tau e_j=-f_j\). The two new coefficients are \(c_i/c_i=1\) and \(c_ic_j=1\). This gives exactly (AB.5).

For the converse, start with any finite-type row Cartan matrix and its algebra from U.1. A permutation \(\pi\) preserving the matrix takes every Serre relation to the identically indexed relation. The extra inverse scalings by \(c_i\) likewise preserve every relation, as checked in Z.3. Thus (AB.5) defines a bracket-preserving linear map, and applying it twice fixes every generator because \(\pi^2=1\) and \(c_ic_{\pi(i)}=1\). It is an involutive automorphism. Its coefficients here are real signs, so direct substitution in (AA.2) proves that it commutes with \(\tau\), and hence restricts to the compact real form.

For a general compact real form with its adapted generators, the U.1 presentation isomorphism intertwines its \(\tau\) with (AA.2). It is therefore an isomorphism on the compact real fixed algebras as well. Formula (AB.5) transports the original involution to one of the listed representatives. For a fixed \(\mathfrak u\), any other identification differs by an automorphism of \(\mathfrak u\); consequently this is the asserted surjectivity onto conjugacy classes, without any uniqueness claim. □

AC. Matrix foundations for the classical involutions

We use \(\mathfrak{sp}(n)\) for the compact algebra of rank \(n\), and \(\mathfrak{sp}_{2n}(\mathbb C)\) or \(\mathfrak{sp}_{2n}(\mathbb R)\) for the algebras preserving a nondegenerate alternating form on a \(2n\)-dimensional vector space over the indicated field. A superscript \(T\) means transpose and \(X^*=\bar X^T\). Put \[ J_n=\begin{pmatrix}0&I_n\\-I_n&0\end{pmatrix}. \tag{AC.1} \]

Theorem AC.1 (the classical compact forms and their complete root decompositions). The following compact real algebras have the indicated complexifications, roots and real dimensions:

Compact algebra Complexification Roots Dimension
\(\mathfrak{su}(N)\), \(N\geq2\) \(\mathfrak{sl}_N(\mathbb C)\) \(\varepsilon_i-\varepsilon_j,\ i\ne j\), on \(\sum t_i=0\) \(N^2-1\)
\(\mathfrak{so}(2n+1)\), \(n\geq1\) \(\mathfrak{so}_{2n+1}(\mathbb C)\) \(\pm\varepsilon_i\pm\varepsilon_j\ (i<j),\ \pm\varepsilon_i\) \(n(2n+1)\)
\(\mathfrak{sp}(n)\), \(n\geq1\) \(\mathfrak{sp}_{2n}(\mathbb C)\) \(\pm\varepsilon_i\pm\varepsilon_j\ (i<j),\ \pm2\varepsilon_i\) \(n(2n+1)\)
\(\mathfrak{so}(2n)\), \(n\geq2\) \(\mathfrak{so}_{2n}(\mathbb C)\) \(\pm\varepsilon_i\pm\varepsilon_j\ (i<j)\) \(n(2n-1)\)

Here \(\mathfrak{su}(N)=\{X:X^*=-X,\ \operatorname{tr}X=0\}\), \(\mathfrak{sp}(n)=\{X:X^*=-X,\ X^TJ_n+J_nX=0\}\), and \(\mathfrak{so}(M)\) consists of real skew-symmetric \(M\)-by-\(M\) matrices. Each listed algebra is semisimple and has negative-definite Killing form. The types are \(A_{N-1},B_n,C_n,D_n\), respectively, with \(B_1=C_1=A_1\), \(D_2=A_1\oplus A_1\), and \(D_3=A_3\). Every root space is one dimensional. The commutant of each complex algebra on its displayed defining vector space consists only of scalar matrices.

Proof. For \(\mathfrak{sl}_N\), take trace-zero diagonal matrices \(H=\operatorname{diag}(t_1,\ldots,t_N)\). The matrix unit \(E_{ij}\), \(i\ne j\), has weight \(t_i-t_j\), since \([H,E_{ij}]=(t_i-t_j)E_{ij}\). The zero-weight subspace is exactly the diagonal trace-zero space. These matrices are a basis, giving all weights, their multiplicities and the dimension.

For the symplectic case, solving \(X^TJ_n+J_nX=0\) entry by entry gives \[ X=\begin{pmatrix}A&B\\C&-A^T\end{pmatrix}, \qquad B^T=B,\quad C^T=C. \tag{AC.2} \] Take \(H=\operatorname{diag}(t_1,\ldots,t_n,-t_1,\ldots,-t_n)\). The off-diagonal entries of \(A\) give the vectors \(E_{ij}-E_{n+j,n+i}\) of weight \(\varepsilon_i-\varepsilon_j\). For \(i<j\), the entries of \(B\) give \(E_{i,n+j}+E_{j,n+i}\) of weight \(\varepsilon_i+\varepsilon_j\); its diagonal entries give \(E_{i,n+i}\) of weight \(2\varepsilon_i\). The entries of \(C\) give the corresponding negative weights. Diagonal \(A\) is the entire zero-weight space. This proves the complete root list and the dimension \(n^2+2n(n+1)/2=n(2n+1)\).

For the even orthogonal case, choose a complex basis in which the symmetric bilinear form has matrix \[ S=\begin{pmatrix}0&I_n\\I_n&0\end{pmatrix}. \] Such a basis is obtained from real orthonormal pairs \(a_i,b_i\) by \(z_i=(a_i+ib_i)/\sqrt2,\ w_i=(a_i-ib_i)/\sqrt2\). It is also unitary for the usual positive Hermitian form. The equation \(X^TS+SX=0\) again gives (AC.2), but now \(B^T=-B,\ C^T=-C\). Replace the plus signs in the off-diagonal \(B,C\) root vectors by minus signs, and omit their diagonal entries. Thus the only roots are \(\pm\varepsilon_i\pm\varepsilon_j\), \(i<j\), and the dimension is \(n^2+n(n-1)=n(2n-1)\).

For the odd orthogonal case add a real unit vector \(v_0\), orthogonal to the preceding pairs. With the ordered coordinates \(z,w,v_0\), the form is \(\operatorname{diag}(S,1)\), and solving its infinitesimal preservation equation gives \[ X=\begin{pmatrix} A&B&u\\ C&-A^T&v\\-v^T&-u^T&0 \end{pmatrix},\qquad B^T=-B,\quad C^T=-C. \tag{AC.3} \] The previous weights persist. The vector \(u_i\) contributes \(E_{i,0}-E_{0,n+i}\) of weight \(\varepsilon_i\), and \(v_i\) contributes \(E_{n+i,0}-E_{0,i}\) of weight \(-\varepsilon_i\); the index \(0\) here denotes the last coordinate. There are no other entries. The dimension is \(n(2n-1)+2n=n(2n+1)\). For \(n=1\), only these last two root spaces occur.

The map \(\tau X=-X^*\) preserves each displayed complex algebra and is a conjugation. For (AC.2) this follows by transposing the symmetric block conditions. For the orthogonal models, the preceding unitary change of basis reduces the assertion to real skew-symmetric matrices, where \(-X^*=\bar X\). Its fixed algebras are precisely the compact algebras in the table. Formula (AA.1) proves that their complexifications are the full displayed complex algebras.

Before using semisimple root theory, we can verify semisimplicity directly. The real form \[ Q(X,Y)=-\operatorname{Re}\operatorname{tr}(XY) \tag{AC.4} \] is positive definite on skew-Hermitian matrices and invariant under brackets by cyclicity of trace. The centre of each complex algebra is zero: a central element must lie in the zero-weight diagonal space, and the listed roots span its dual, forcing every diagonal coordinate to vanish. For \(D_2\), the two independent roots \(\varepsilon_1-\varepsilon_2,\varepsilon_1+\varepsilon_2\) still span. Thus the real centre is also zero. Every adjoint operator on the compact algebra is skew for \(Q\), and for an orthonormal real basis \(v_j\), \[ K(X,X)=-\sum_j\|[X,v_j]\|_Q^2<0\quad(X\ne0). \tag{AC.5} \] Symmetric spaces H.5–H.6 prove semisimplicity over both fields.

The displayed diagonal algebra is toral and equals its centralizer, so is a Cartan. The Killing form on it is the sum of products of root values. In the four cases it is, respectively, \[ 2N\sum_i t_i s_i,\quad 2(2n-1)\sum_i t_i s_i,\quad 4(n+1)\sum_i t_i s_i,\quad 4(n-1)\sum_i t_i s_i. \tag{AC.6} \] For \(A\), expand \(\sum_{i\ne j}(t_i-t_j)(s_i-s_j)\) and use both zero sums. For the other types, the four roots for each pair \(i<j\) cancel mixed products and contribute \(4t_is_i+4t_js_j\). The extra roots contribute \(2t_is_i\) for \(B\) and \(8t_is_i\) for \(C\). Thus their canonical root metrics are positive multiples of the ordinary coordinate metrics. The complete coordinate simple-basis and Cartan calculations in De Rham decomposition AE.3, with the row convention of P.1, identify the types. The rank-one identifications are immediate from a single opposite pair. \(D_2\) is the orthogonal union of its two opposite pairs. For \(D_3\), the basis \(\varepsilon_1-\varepsilon_2,\ \varepsilon_2-\varepsilon_3,\ \varepsilon_2+\varepsilon_3\) has a chain diagram with three vertices, hence type \(A_3\). U.1 then gives the asserted Lie-algebra identifications. A connected diagram gives simplicity by T.4; \(D_2\) has its two simple summands.

Finally let a matrix \(M\) commute with the complex algebra. Commuting with the diagonal Cartan makes \(M\) diagonal, because all its weights on the defining vector space are distinct linear functionals: \(t_i\) for \(A\), the \(t_i,-t_i\) for \(C,D\), and these together with zero for \(B\). The finite-hyperplane argument in De Rham decomposition AD.5 gives a Cartan element separating these finitely many weights, so the assertion follows entry by entry. A nonzero entry of any root matrix then forces the two corresponding diagonal entries of \(M\) to agree. The \(A\) matrix units connect every pair. In \(C\), the \(A\) entries connect positive coordinates to one another and negative coordinates to one another, and \(E_{i,n+i}\) joins the two sets. In \(D\), the \(B\) entries join them for \(n\geq2\). In \(B\), the short-root entries join both sets through the last coordinate, also when \(n=1\). Thus all entries agree and \(M\) is scalar. □

Lemma AC.2 (real, quaternionic and symplectic linear algebra). An antiunitary map \(j\) on a finite-dimensional complex Hermitian space with \(j^2=1\) is conjugate by a unitary basis change to ordinary conjugation. If \(j^2=-1\), the dimension is even, and in a unitary basis \(j=J_n\kappa\), where \(\kappa\) is entrywise conjugation. The complex-linear operators commuting with \(J_n\kappa\) are exactly \[ \Phi(A+B\mathbf j)= \begin{pmatrix}A&B\\-\bar B&\bar A\end{pmatrix}, \tag{AC.7} \] the matrix realization of quaternionic matrices. It preserves products and adjoints. In particular, \[ \mathfrak{sp}(n)=\{Q\in M_n(\mathbb H):Q^\dagger=-Q\}, \tag{AC.8} \] and the complex trace in (AC.7) is \(2\operatorname{Re}\operatorname{tr}_{\mathbb H}Q\).

Proof. If \(j^2=1\), formula (AA.1), applied to the vector space, writes it as \(V_{\mathbb R}+iV_{\mathbb R}\), where \(V_{\mathbb R}\) is the fixed real space. The Hermitian product is real on \(V_{\mathbb R}\), since antiunitarity conjugates it. Real Gram–Schmidt gives an orthonormal real basis there; it is a complex basis of the whole space, and \(j\) is conjugation in that basis.

If \(j^2=-1\), a unit vector \(v\) is orthogonal to \(jv\). Indeed antiunitarity applied to \(v,jv\) gives \(\langle jv,j^2v\rangle=\overline{\langle v,jv\rangle}\), whereas \(j^2v=-v\) makes the left side its negative. The complex span of \(v,jv\) is \(j\)-stable. Its orthogonal complement is also \(j\)-stable, by antiunitarity. Induction produces orthonormal pairs; choose the order and sign in each pair so that the matrix is \(J_n\kappa\). This also proves even dimension. Any two such paired orthonormal bases are related by a unitary operator commuting with \(j\).

For completeness, define \(\mathbb H\) as the real algebra of pairs \(a+b\mathbf j\), \(a,b\in\mathbb C\), with multiplication \[ (a+b\mathbf j)(c+d\mathbf j) =(ac-b\bar d)+(ad+b\bar c)\mathbf j. \tag{AC.9} \] The injective map \[ a+b\mathbf j\longmapsto \begin{pmatrix}a&b\\-\bar b&\bar a\end{pmatrix} \] respects multiplication by direct multiplication of its four entries. It proves associativity. It also gives \(\mathbf j^2=-1,\ \mathbf j a=\bar a\mathbf j\), and conjugation \(\overline{a+b\mathbf j}=\bar a-b\mathbf j\) corresponds to conjugate transpose. The product with this conjugate is \((|a|^2+|b|^2)I\), so every nonzero quaternion is invertible. Applying the entry formula to matrices proves (AC.7), product preservation and adjoint preservation; here \(\dagger\) means quaternionic conjugate transpose. Solving \(XJ_n=J_n\bar X\) in four blocks gives exactly (AC.7).

For \(X\in\mathfrak{sp}_{2n}(\mathbb C)\), the defining equation gives \[ J_n\bar XJ_n^{-1}=-X^*. \tag{AC.10} \] Thus within that complex algebra, commuting with \(J_n\kappa\) is equivalent to being skew-Hermitian. Conversely a skew-Hermitian matrix commuting with \(J_n\kappa\) satisfies the symplectic equation by reversing this calculation. This proves (AC.8). Taking diagonal traces in (AC.7) proves the final trace assertion.

We record two elementary basis constructions that will be used below. A real orthogonal operator with square \(1\) has orthogonal \(+1,-1\) eigenspaces: its inverse equals itself, so it is self-adjoint and the real spectral theorem, De Rham decomposition H.2, applies. A real orthogonal operator with square \(-1\) is skew-adjoint; a real unit vector \(v\) and its image are orthogonal, and the orthogonal complement of their plane is invariant. Induction gives the usual two-dimensional rotation blocks. Finally a nondegenerate alternating real bilinear form admits a symplectic basis: choose \(v,w\) with their pairing one; subtract their two pairing components from any vector to obtain the orthogonal complement of their plane for the alternating form. This is a direct sum, its complementary restriction is nondegenerate, and induction gives all the pairs. These constructions prove the asserted normal forms without a classification theorem about forms. □

Lemma AC.3 (toral phases and compact reflection representatives). Fix the normalized generators and compact conjugation of AA.1. Every automorphism fixing the Cartan pointwise and preserving the compact real form is \(\exp(\operatorname{ad}T)\) for some \(T\in\mathfrak t=\mathfrak h\cap\mathfrak u\). Every compact involution is conjugate under \(\operatorname{Aut}_{\mathbb R}(\mathfrak u)\) to the product of such a toral automorphism and a diagram permutation of order at most two. Each simple reflection is induced on \(\mathfrak h\) by the compact inner automorphism \[ n_i=\exp\bigl(\tfrac{\pi}{2}\operatorname{ad}(e_i-f_i)\bigr). \tag{AC.11} \] For a toral involution with generator signs \(c_j=\pm1\), conjugation by \(n_i\) changes them to \[ c'_j=c_jc_i^{-a_{ij}}. \tag{AC.12} \]

Proof. A Cartan-pointwise automorphism acts on the one-dimensional simple root spaces by \(e_i\mapsto c_ie_i\). The bracket relation gives \(f_i\mapsto c_i^{-1}f_i\), and compactness preservation gives \(|c_i|=1\), as in AB.2. Write \(c_i=e^{i\theta_i}\). The simple roots are a basis of \(\mathfrak h_{\mathbb R}^*\), where \(\mathfrak h_{\mathbb R}\) is the real span of the \(h_i\), by Q.5. Solve \(\alpha_i(H)=\theta_i\) there and put \(T=iH\in\mathfrak t\). Then \(\exp(\operatorname{ad}T)\) has exactly these actions on all generators. Since the generators generate \(\mathfrak g\), the automorphisms agree. The elementary unit-circle parameterization used here is Connections E.1.

AB.2 supplies adapted normalized generators for any compact involution. U.1 identifies this presentation with the fixed one, intertwining the compact conjugations because their values on the generators agree. The resulting identification restricts to a compact real automorphism. In these coordinates, remove the pure diagram permutation from the involution; the remainder fixes \(\mathfrak h\) pointwise and is the toral automorphism just proved. This proves the product assertion; it does not assert a unique product or unique conjugacy representative. More generally any other compact automorphism inducing the same permutation of these simple roots can replace the pure diagram map: their quotient fixes the Cartan pointwise, so their difference is again a toral factor.

The vector \(e_i-f_i\) lies in \(\mathfrak u\). For any real derivation \(D\), differentiating \(e^{-tD}[e^{tD}X,e^{tD}Y]\) gives zero by the derivation rule. The finite-dimensional linear ODE and its exponential solution are Local tools 2.3. Hence its adjoint exponential preserves brackets and \(\mathfrak u\). On the span of \(h_i,e_i+f_i\), its adjoint sends \[ h_i\longmapsto-2(e_i+f_i),\qquad e_i+f_i\longmapsto2h_i. \] The exponential at time \(\pi/2\) therefore sends \(h_i\) to \(-h_i\). If \(H\in\ker\alpha_i\), it commutes with \(e_i,f_i\) and is fixed. Decomposing \(H\) into this kernel and \(\alpha_i(H)h_i/2\) proves the reflection formula \(H\mapsto H-\alpha_i(H)h_i\). Products of the \(n_i\) consequently realize every element of the Weyl group.

The action of a toral automorphism on a root \(\alpha=\sum_jm_j\alpha_j\) is the scalar \(\prod_jc_j^{m_j}\): this follows either from its exponential formula or from the generating root brackets in U.1. Conjugating by \(n_i\) replaces \(\alpha_j\) by \(s_i\alpha_j=\alpha_j-a_{ij}\alpha_i\), which gives (AC.12). In particular, when \(c_i=-1\), exactly the neighbours with odd row entry \(a_{ij}\) change sign. This is a proved conjugacy operation; no assertion that these operations give all equivalences is needed here. □

AD. Classification of the classical compact involutions

For a compact involution \(\sigma\), write \(\mathfrak k=\mathfrak u_+\), \(\mathfrak p=\mathfrak u_-\), and call \(\mathfrak k+i\mathfrak p\) its real dual. This is the real form proved in AA.5. In the following statements, conjugacy means conjugacy under all real automorphisms of the compact algebra.

Theorem AD.1 (unitary algebras). Every involution of \(\mathfrak{su}(N)\), \(N\geq2\), is conjugate to one of the following, including the identity case in the first row:

Kind Fixed algebra \(\mathfrak k\) Real dual \(\dim_{\mathbb R}\mathfrak p\)
Unitary signs, \(p+q=N\) \(\mathfrak s(\mathfrak u(p)\oplus\mathfrak u(q))\) \(\mathfrak{su}(p,q)\) \(2pq\)
Real structure \(\mathfrak{so}(N)\) \(\mathfrak{sl}_N(\mathbb R)\) \((N-1)(N+2)/2\)
Quaternionic structure, \(N=2m\) \(\mathfrak{sp}(m)\) \(\mathfrak{su}^*(2m)\) \((m-1)(2m+1)\)

The representatives in row order are \(\operatorname{Ad}\operatorname{diag}(I_p,-I_q)\), \(X\mapsto\bar X\), and \(X\mapsto J_m\bar XJ_m^{-1}\) on \(\mathfrak{su}(N)\). Here \(\mathfrak s\) imposes zero total complex trace, \(\mathfrak{su}^*(2m)=\mathfrak{sl}_m(\mathbb H)\), and \(\mathfrak{sl}_m(\mathbb H)\) means quaternionic matrices with zero real part of the quaternionic trace. In the first row \(p,q\geq0\), and interchanging them does not change the involution class. For \(N=2\), the second row repeats the first row with \(p=q=1\), and the third row is the identity. For \(N\geq3\), the displayed nonidentity rows, with unordered \(p,q\), are distinct.

Proof. The \(A_{N-1}\) diagram is a chain. For \(N\geq3\) its only permutations preserving the Cartan matrix are the identity and reversal; for \(N=2\) there is only the identity. Use the standard generators \(e_i=E_{i,i+1}, f_i=E_{i+1,i}, h_i=E_{ii}-E_{i+1,i+1}\). The map \(\gamma(X)=-X^T\) preserves \(\mathfrak{sl}_N\), since transposition reverses products; on its compact real form it is \(X\mapsto\bar X\). Let \(R\) be the real antidiagonal matrix with antidiagonal entries \(R_{i,N+1-i}=(-1)^{i-1}\). It is unitary, \(R^2=(-1)^{N-1}I\), and direct substitution gives \[ \operatorname{Ad}R\circ\gamma: \quad e_i\mapsto e_{N-i},\quad f_i\mapsto f_{N-i},\quad h_i\mapsto h_{N-i}. \tag{AD.1} \] For instance conjugation of \(E_{i+1,i}\) by \(R\) gives \(-E_{N-i,N+1-i}\), canceling the minus sign in \(\gamma\). Thus (AD.1) realizes diagram reversal.

By AC.3, every involution is conjugate to \(\operatorname{Ad}U\) or \(\operatorname{Ad}U\circ\gamma\) for a unitary matrix \(U\): the toral factor is conjugation by the diagonal unitary matrix \(\exp T\), and (AD.1) gives the other possibility. We now classify these two kinds directly.

For \(\sigma=\operatorname{Ad}U\), its square is the identity precisely when \(U^2\) commutes with \(\mathfrak{sl}_N\). By AC.1, \(U^2=\lambda I\), with \(|\lambda|=1\). Multiply \(U\) by a unit scalar whose square is \(\lambda^{-1}\); this does not change \(\operatorname{Ad}U\) and makes \(U^2=I\). Such a unitary is self-adjoint. K.1 gives orthogonal \(+1,-1\) eigenspaces of dimensions \(p,q\), so unitary conjugation makes \(U=\operatorname{diag}(I_p,-I_q)\). Replacing \(U\) by \(-U\) interchanges the dimensions and leaves its adjoint action unchanged.

The even part consists of block-diagonal skew-Hermitian matrices of zero total trace. The odd part consists of \[ X_Z=\begin{pmatrix}0&Z\\-Z^*&0\end{pmatrix}, \qquad Z\in M_{p,q}(\mathbb C). \tag{AD.2} \] Multiplication by \(i\) in the dual replaces these by all blocks \(\begin{psmallmatrix}0&W\\W^*&0\end{psmallmatrix}\), by putting \(W=iZ\). Together with the same even part, these are exactly the trace-zero matrices satisfying \[ X^*S+SX=0,\qquad S=\operatorname{diag}(I_p,-I_q). \] This defines \(\mathfrak{su}(p,q)\). The odd real dimension is \(2pq\).

For \(\sigma=\operatorname{Ad}U\circ\gamma\), the formula on \(\mathfrak{su}(N)\) is \(X\mapsto U\bar XU^{-1}\). Let \(j=U\kappa\), an antiunitary operator on \(\mathbb C^N\). The condition \(\sigma^2=1\) and AC.1 give \(j^2=U\bar U=\lambda I\), where \(|\lambda|=1\). Conjugating this equality entrywise gives \(\bar U U=\bar\lambda I\); conjugating the latter by \(U\) gives \(U\bar U=\bar\lambda I\), so \(\lambda=\bar\lambda\) and \(\lambda=\pm1\).

If \(j^2=1\), AC.2 puts \(j=\kappa\) in a unitary basis. Then \(\mathfrak k\) consists of real skew-symmetric matrices, namely \(\mathfrak{so}(N)\). On all of \(\mathfrak{sl}_N(\mathbb C)\), the compact conjugation is \(\tau X=-X^*\), while the complex extension of \(\sigma\) is \(-X^T\). Their product is entrywise conjugation. Thus the real dual is \(\mathfrak{sl}_N(\mathbb R)\), with odd dimension \[ (N^2-1)-N(N-1)/2=(N-1)(N+2)/2. \] If \(j^2=-1\), AC.2 gives \(N=2m\) and \(j=J_m\kappa\). Its commuting skew-Hermitian matrices are \(\mathfrak{sp}(m)\). The product \(\sigma\tau\) on the complex algebra is \(X\mapsto J_m\bar XJ_m^{-1}\), so its fixed matrices are (AC.7) with complex trace zero, equivalently zero real quaternionic trace. This is the stated \(\mathfrak{sl}_m(\mathbb H)\), also denoted \(\mathfrak{su}^*(2m)\). Its odd dimension is \[ (4m^2-1)-m(2m+1)=(m-1)(2m+1). \]

These arguments prove exhaustion. For \(N\geq3\), the nontrivial first-row fixed algebra has one-dimensional centre: its central elements are \(\operatorname{diag}(iaI_p,ibI_q)\) with \(pa+qb=0\). This follows by commuting with the matrix units in each block; it remains valid if a block has size one. The other two fixed algebras have zero centre by AC.1, so cannot be conjugate to this row. When both the second and third rows occur, their fixed dimensions are respectively \(m(2m-1)\) and \(m(2m+1)\), which are different. Within the first row, the odd dimension \(2p(N-p)\) determines the unordered pair \(p,q\); it is strictly increasing for \(0\leq p\leq N/2\), since \((p+1)(N-p-1)-p(N-p)=N-2p-1>0\) before the last distinct value. The identity has zero odd part and is separate.

For \(N=2\), direct multiplication on \(e,f,h\) gives \(\gamma=\operatorname{Ad}J_1\). Multiplication of \(J_1\) by \(i\) gives a Hermitian unitary with eigenvalues \(1,-1\), so this is the first row with \(p=q=1\). Composing once more with \(\operatorname{Ad}J_1\) gives the identity, which is the third row at \(m=1\). This proves the boundary identifications. □

Theorem AD.2 (symplectic algebras). Every involution of \(\mathfrak{sp}(n)\) is conjugate to one of the following:

Kind Fixed algebra Real dual Odd dimension
Quaternionic signs, \(p+q=n\) \(\mathfrak{sp}(p)\oplus\mathfrak{sp}(q)\) \(\mathfrak{sp}(p,q)\) \(4pq\)
Complex structure \(\mathfrak u(n)\) \(\mathfrak{sp}_{2n}(\mathbb R)\) \(n(n+1)\)

The first representative is conjugation by \(+1\) on \(p\) quaternionic coordinates and \(-1\) on \(q\); the second is \(\operatorname{Ad}\operatorname{diag}(iI_n,-iI_n)\) in the complex realization. In the first row \(p,q\geq0\), their interchange is redundant, and a zero parameter gives the identity. The notation \(\mathfrak{sp}(p,q)\) means quaternionic matrices satisfying \[ X^\dagger S+SX=0,\qquad S=\operatorname{diag}(I_p,-I_q). \tag{AD.3} \] The two nonidentity kinds are distinct, and the unordered pair \(p,q\) distinguishes the first-row classes.

Proof. The \(C_n\) diagram has no nonidentity Cartan-matrix permutation: its end arrow distinguishes the two ends when \(n\geq2\), and for \(n=1\) there is one vertex. Thus AC.3 makes every involution conjugate to \(\operatorname{Ad}U\), where \(U=\exp T\) is both unitary and complex symplectic. It commutes with \(j=J_n\kappa\). Its square commutes with the defining complex algebra, so AC.1 gives \(U^2=\lambda I\). Since \(U^2\) preserves \(J_n\), one has \(\lambda^2=1\).

If \(U^2=1\), its orthogonal complex \(+1,-1\) eigenspaces are \(j\)-stable, because \(Uj=jU\). By AC.2 they have even dimensions \(2p,2q\), and each has a paired orthonormal basis. Joining these bases is a unitary change commuting with \(j\), hence symplectic: for a unitary \(V\), the equation \(VJ_n=J_n\bar V\) is equivalent to \(V^TJ_nV=J_n\), by multiplying and using \(V^*V=I\). It is therefore an allowed compact matrix conjugation. In quaternionic coordinates, \(U\) is now \(\operatorname{diag}(I_p,-I_q)\). Its fixed skew-Hermitian quaternionic matrices are the two diagonal \(\mathfrak{sp}\) blocks. Its odd part consists of \[ X_B=\begin{pmatrix}0&B\\-B^\dagger&0\end{pmatrix}, \qquad B\in M_{p,q}(\mathbb H). \tag{AD.4} \]

To identify the dual without confusing the complexification scalar \(i\) with quaternionic left multiplication, use the real linear map \[ \begin{pmatrix}A&0\\0&D\end{pmatrix}+iX_B \longmapsto \begin{pmatrix}A&B\\B^\dagger&D\end{pmatrix}. \tag{AD.5} \] Its target is exactly (AD.3), with \(A^\dagger=-A,\ D^\dagger=-D\). It preserves even-even brackets. Both even-odd brackets send \(B\) to \(AB-BD\). Finally \[ [X_B,X_C]= \operatorname{diag}(-BC^\dagger+CB^\dagger,\, -B^\dagger C+C^\dagger B). \tag{AD.6} \] The bracket of the two positive-lower-block matrices on the right of (AD.5) is the negative of (AD.6), exactly the sign supplied by \(i^2=-1\) on the left. Thus (AD.5) is an isomorphism of real Lie algebras. The odd dimension is \(4pq\).

If \(U^2=-1\), its eigenvalues are \(i,-i\). Since \(j\) is antilinear and commutes with \(U\), it exchanges these eigenspaces, which are orthogonal by unitary diagonalization from K.1. Choose an orthonormal basis in the \(i\)-eigenspace and its paired images under \(j\). Both eigenspaces have dimension \(n\), and the resulting compact symplectic basis change puts \(U=\operatorname{diag}(iI_n,-iI_n)\). In (AC.2), fixedness forces \(B=C=0\); the compact condition then says \(A^*=-A\). Therefore \(\mathfrak k\simeq\mathfrak u(n)\).

For the real dual, (AC.10) identifies \(\tau\) on the complex algebra with conjugation by the antiunitary \(j\). Hence \(\sigma\tau\) is conjugation by \(c=Uj\). Its square on vectors is \(c^2=U^2j^2=1\). Let \(W\) be the real fixed space of \(c\). By AC.2 its complexification is the defining \(\mathbb C^{2n}\). The complex alternating form \(\beta(v,w)=v^TJ_nw\) satisfies \[ \beta(jv,jw)=\overline{\beta(v,w)},\qquad \beta(cv,cw)=\overline{\beta(v,w)}, \] the first by \(J_n^TJ_n=I\), and the second because \(U\) is symplectic. Thus \(\beta|_W\) is real. It is nondegenerate: a vector of \(W\) annihilating \(W\) also annihilates \(W+iW\), hence vanishes. The real symplectic-basis construction in AC.2 identifies its infinitesimal preservers with \(\mathfrak{sp}_{2n}(\mathbb R)\). These are precisely the complex symplectic matrices commuting with \(c\), namely the real dual. Subtracting fixed dimensions gives \(n(2n+1)-n^2=n(n+1)\).

The nontrivial first-row fixed algebra is centre-free by AC.1, whereas \(\mathfrak u(n)\) has centre \(i\mathbb RI_n\), so the rows are distinct. Within the first row, \(4p(n-p)\) determines the unordered pair by the same strict-increase calculation as in AD.1. A quaternionic coordinate permutation interchanges the blocks, and replacing \(U\) by \(-U\) leaves its adjoint action unchanged. The identity is exactly the zero-odd-part case. □

Theorem AD.3 (orthogonal algebras). For \(N\geq3\), every involution of \(\mathfrak{so}(N)\), up to all compact-algebra automorphisms, has one of the following representatives:

Kind Fixed algebra Real dual Odd dimension
Orthogonal signs, \(p+q=N\) \(\mathfrak{so}(p)\oplus\mathfrak{so}(q)\) \(\mathfrak{so}(p,q)\) \(pq\)
Complex structure, \(N=2n\) \(\mathfrak u(n)\) \(\mathfrak{so}^*(2n)\) \(n(n-1)\)

The representatives are respectively \(\operatorname{Ad}\operatorname{diag}(I_p,-I_q)\) and \(\operatorname{Ad}J_n\). The first row permits zero parameters and is unchanged by interchanging them.
